IiVOMF.N'S CHAT.

The Best-known Spot in Londosi.—The Marble Arch at one of the entrances to Hyde Park, which is familiar to most visitors to London, was intended for the main entrance to Buckingham Palace, but was found to be unsuitable for this purpose and was transferred to its present site in 1851. It is now proposed to set back further in the Park, in the centre of a crescent drive,as part of a contemplated improvement scheme. The arch is of Carrar marble, and cost over £7,000. It is said to be the largAt work of mere ornament in the British Isles, excluding memorials commemorative of a great name or cause, but it sinks into insignificance when compared with the Arc de Triomphe, which stands at the top of the Champs Elysdes, in Paris. That was commenced by Napoleon in 1806, but was not com- pleted until thirty years later, and cost altogether nearly half a million sterling. A Royal Sportswomam—Princess Patricia of Connaught is frequently spoken of as our "open-air Princess," and not without reason. Princess Patricia is a fearless rider, a capital pedestrian, and a swift runner, she is moreover devoted to tennis' hockey, and golf, particularly the latter, which royal and ancient game her Royal Highness plays regularly, regardless of the weather. The Duchess of Skania, who with her husband and two little sons has been slaying at Bagshot Park on a visit to the Duke and Duchess of Connaught, is also fond of open-air pursuits, but since her marriage she has of course had much less time at her dispasal in which to follow them. Lady Savile.—On Monday last,before leaving Rufford Abbey, the King planted a mulberry tree, which makes the eighth of its kind planted by His Majesty, in Lady Saviles Italian garden. Lady Savile, who for many years has been honoured with the friendship of the King, is a daughter of the late Mr. Charles Webster, Wedderburn, and was the widow of Mr. H. Helgariwhen she married Lord Savile, some thirteen years back. Lady Savile is a perfect hostess possessing great tack and conversational powers, and is always perfectly dressed. Black is Lady Savile's favourite wear, and with her wealth of bright golden hair, the sable hue suits her perfectly. The Painter of" The Cheat."—As yet the effect of the Act, legalising marriage . with a deceased wife's sister, has been chiefly retrospective, for there does not appear to have been a great rush of widowers te marry their sister-in-law. Many of course became naturalised subjects of other countries in order to contract such a marriage, and among others effected by the Act is the Hon. John Collier, the artist. who is married to a younger sister of his former wife. He is a brother of Lord Monkswell, and is the well-known painter of that ..famous picture "The Cheat,"—which excited so much interest, owing to the problem which of the two women was thi3 cheat. It was never intended to be a problem picture, and inject suggests little knowledge of the practice of cheating at cards. The lady standitbo, up in the picture is represented as having turned down a marked card. That is all, but of course if people cheat at bridge, there are more effective and less risky ways of doing so, than that of using marked cards, which is the very last method they would adopt. Men's Fashions.—Hitherto the fashions which govern men's dress have been limited to a few alterations, generally going from one extreme to the other. Trousers, for instance, will be very tight or very baggy, but whereas a feminine fashion may last only six weeks, trousers will be either one way or the other for six years or more, and that, it must be admitted, is a dis- tinct advantage over a system of constant changes. It is because men's clothes offer so few, opportunities for changing that their style has to last so long, and some of them are not at all becoming, such as is the one that has transformed the lounge coat into a sort of mongrel garment half-jacket and half-jacket and half-morning coat. Still men, making no trouble of these things or of the wearing of high collars, and having taken kindly to the fancy waistcoat are now urged to go in for velvets and velveteens, new styles of fancy coloured hats and in other ways to elaborate their personal adornment. In fact they are to set up a sort of Dame Fashion of their own but they should remember before- hand, that the dictates of such an autocratic person generally involves no little sacrifice of comfort._ and convenience. At the Tailors.—From the medium three-quarter coat, we are creeping on to the longer varieties, which is a matter for regret, as unless our inches exceed those of the average woman, and our skirts are long, the effect is not good. Another pitfall we should avoid, when at the tailore, is the excessively short walking skirt. Such a garment should cell clear the ground of course, but in running to violent extremes in this direction, we decidedly lay ourselves open to being classed among loud and vulgar dressers. Waistcoats.—Coats are being built very open at the neck,waistcoats therefore will be of immense importance during the coming winter. In shopland, the selection of dressy waistcoats, suitable for wear with or tailor-mades, is wonderful, and few of us will feel inclined to braid, embroider paint, or otherwise spend our time, in creating these dainty and fascinating items. Very beantiful and costly buttons adorn all the best waistcoats, enamel, old paste, and quartz set in gun metal, being among the most approved. Detachable Plounces.—Many and various are the methods devised for fastening detachable flounces to our underskirts, but perhaps the most effective is that of lacing them together. Another excellent device CC/Mists of the closely vandyked band, which is attached to the skirt, the vandykes buttoning down on to the flounce. Needless to say, there must be no " sagging " anywhere, and the time expended in sewing on buttons and making buttonholes and eyelet holes, is indeed well spent, when the petticoat with detachable flounces is concerned. For the skirt portion, a short length of brocade should be indulged in, this fabric, provided it is of home manufacture, being very nearly "everlasting wear." Increased Household Bills.—*On all -sides, the pur- veyors of household necessities are following the example of the coal merchant, in putting up prices. Not only fuel, but bread, milk and in fact nearly all the prime necessaries of life are costing more and it is estimated that in working class families this means a reduction of twenty per cent on the purchasing power of the weekly household allowance. To some extent the general rise in prices is no doubt due to abnormal conditions of isupply and demand, but it is a little singular that these periods of dear times always coincide with the oncoming of autumn and winter, when the people are less able to go on short commons than in the spring and slimmer. In the old days, when the country was dependent upon home supplies, articles of food were always dearer in the winter, but nowadays we are independent of seasons, and there is more than a suspicion that the desire to extract larger profits from the public is at the bottom of the general rise id prices. When coal was excessively dear, a few years ago. two or three of the largest. Town Councils proposed to buy, and work mines to supply the public at cost price, and it would perhaps be well for the dealers to bear in mind that municipal competition is more likely to be carried into effect now than it was then. The Blackberry.—Various are the names given to the fruit of the bramble. In some parts of the country it is called the dewberry, and in the Scottish Highlands it goes by the name of the cloud-berry, or mountain bramble. Scald-besd is another name given to the blackberry, owing to its supposed tendency to produce the eruption known as scald-bead in chUdren. This, however, is quite an erroneous idea, for doctots and scientists are agreed that the blackberry is one of the most wholesome fruits, end it was considered by the Greeks to be a preventative of gout. It makes very good jam, and if housewives will take the trouble to make it, it filso furnishes an, excellent and wholesome home made wine.

This practical coat-and-skirt costume is suit- able for hopsack or fine serge; or, if intended for smart occasions, it could be carried out in dark-green faced cloth. The skirt is a simple, well-gored shape, turned up at the hem • with a double row of stitching. The coat is semi-fitting, and is given a slightly short-waisted affect by

_ tried to bring him to the ground ;ibut Talbot had got his feet well apart and was able not only to hold his ground, but to get a good grip. They swayed this way and that, firmly locked, their faces so close that Talbot, with a sickening sense of loathing, could feel on his cheek the man's horrible breath, see his bloodshot eyes glaring into his own. It was a terrible struggle, one that looked as if it were for life or death; and Gibbon from his place behind a tree watched it w:th a sinister enjoyment. He was )being compensated for years of insolence. He expected every moment to see his master go under; but, strangely enough, Talbot seemed able to hold his own ; and Gibbon'senjoyment was beginning to ,be marred by disappointment, when Oatway managed to thtust his leg between Talbot's, and Gibbon drew a long breath as Talbot went down and Oatway, losing his balance, fell on top of him. Gibbon seated himself at the foot of the tree, and, peep- ing round, watched and waited for the denouement with a satisfaction no longer alloyed. ' Talbot, half stunned by the fall and the weight of his assailant's body, lay quite still for a time, and Oatway, presumably w.th the idea of making his victory doubly sure, struck the pro,trate man a cruel blow on the head. The blow seemed-to have an effect contrary to that which Outway had intended, for it roused Taibot's blood. He wriggled one side of himself free, his hand slid• in a stealthy fashion to his pocket and fumbled there, and a moment afterwards Oatway sprang upwards with a sharp, guttural cry, clapped one hand to his side, and, beating the air with the other, fell forward on his face as if he had been —stabbed! In his amazement at this sudden change in the performance which he had been enjoying so hugely, Gibbon rose and, took a step forward; then he changed his mind, and, sinking down at full length; Fay quite still, his head raised just sufficiently above the bracken to pedinit him to see the conclusion of the drama. As Oatway fell, Talbot got to his feet and stood, staggering, the knife red now !—still grasped in his hand. For awhile he stood panting heavily, his .eyes fixed straight before him, as if he were still only half conscious of what had occurred; then his eye 3 fell on the Imotionless figure at his feet, his white face went livid, and his mouth worked convulsively. He . looked from the knife to the still form with its hands clutching the bracken, its hideous face; all drawn .and distorted, faintly sliewing, and back again to the knife. He could scarcely rea7isi it. Had he really stabbed the wretch? He had felt for the knife, opened it, dealt the blow, mechanically. He must have hurt the man— perhaps seriously. A nuisance! There would be a—a fuss now! The brute deserved it, of course— but the consequences! He must get him up, bring him to, help him home. He went to Oatway and stirred him with his foot. • "Get up!" he said, hoarsely. "Don't lie there —shamming. Get up! You brought it on your- sAf—you struck me, you fool!" The man did not move, and Talbot, with a mixture of irritation and disgust, bent down and turned him over. In doing so he saw a deep, wet stain on the side of the figure. He shrank back and glanced at his hands—they were wet— red! He bent lower, looked hard at the face-- how white it was, how still and—and calm !—then thrust his hand inside the waistcoat. , The next instant he had started to his feet with a low cry. Great Heavens! It was incredible, but—but the man was dead! He felt for the heart again, felt through the blood that had welled from the wound. There was no movement. The man was dead. and

CORSETS AND FIGURES

Of all silly discussions, that, surely, on tight- lacing has been most foolish, declares the Lady's Pictorial. No one excuses extremely tigh

lacing, but it is quite another matter to con- demn the use of corsets, and the most mistake% policy in the world for mothers to let their daughters encourage " natural " waists. One has grown almost weary of saying that women in all ages have kept their figures under control. :Ind that no woman without a well-defined waist lboks attractive; but distorting the figure by such means as have been suggested in some of the letters contributed to this silly-season corre- spondence is. ridiculous and unnecessary, a well- cut corset giving.' both beauty and suppiart the figure.

REVISING THE LISTS.

Courts in the Rural Districts.

GRAPPENHALL.

Mr. Clement Elphinstone Lloyd, revising barrister attended at the Large Room, Grappenhall, on Tuesday morning to revise the voters list for the Grappenhalt polling district, which includes Grappenhall, Latchford Without, and that part of Warrington which is within the geographical county of Chester. Mr. Joseph Smith represented the Libezals, and Mr. George Dhckworth, assisted by Mr. J. H. Dean. the Conservatives. The assistant overseers present were:—Messrs. C. H. Hoult representing Grappenhall; J. Smith. Latchford With- out ; and H. Lawrence (for Mr. W. H. Woodcock), Warrington. There was only a small attendance of the public, including the Vicar (Rev. NH. V. Pigot). The bnsiness was mainly of a routine characthr. Arthur Dam., .o. of Mr. A. T. DaMti, Knutsford-road, Grappenhall, applied for a lodger vote. Mr. Smith : Is he over 21? Mr. Hoult : Yes.—Mr. Duckworth : We had one applicant aged 72 last year.—Mr. Smith : We cannot be too careful about these juniors.—The vote was allowed.—Mr. Duckworth made two owner- ship claims for Latchford Without,which were allowed. Five similar claims for Grappenhall were made by Mr. Smith, and four were allowed.

STOCKTON HEATH.

In the afternoon NTT% Lloyd held Fa court at tile Hall Cafe Stockton Heath, for the revision of the lists for Stockton Heath, Appleton and Stratton. Lower Walton and Higher Walton. Messrs. Duckworth, Dean, and H. J. Westbrook appeared for the Conservatives, and Mr. Joseph Smith for the Liberals. The follovving assistant overseers were present :—Mr. E. Bradshaw, Stockton Heath ; Mr. S. Ellison, Appleton and Stratton ; Mr. Joseph Whitefleld, Lower Walton ; and Mr. Alfred Monsdale, Higher Walton. For Stockton Heath there were 1S claims and 9 objections; Appleton and Stretton, S claims and one objection ; Lower Walton, 4 claims ' and no objections. There were no claims or objections for Higher Walton. There was no business of public interest.

.

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

LADY LINDSAY'S PAYING GUEST.

BY ROBERT H. SHERARD, Author of "The Iron Cross," "Whispers," "Jaeob Niemand," "A Bartered Honour," "The Ghost's Revenge," "At the Closed Door," Ste.

"Those Sarsaparilla people haven't lost any time in retaliating, I see. A Sarsaparilla yacht is to cruise on the lake all through the summer, and when at moorings is to lie at Waterhead, just opposite Crusoe's Island. I can imagine Lady Lindsay's feelings, can't you ?—stuck-up Old thing!" It was a Miss Gordon who brought this piece of news, and the announcement of it seemed%to give great pleasure to the ladies assembled at the At Home where the words were uttered. "Though what she has got to be so conceited about," continued Miss Gordon—who was an old maid of great asperity of countenance—"l have never been able to discover. Her husband was only a paltry knight, and--" "Oh, come, Miss Gordon," interrupted a kindly- looking old lady. "You mustn't speak of the late Sir Harold Lindsay like that. He was one of our greatest travellers, and his book on Thibet--" "Yes; we all know about his book on Thibet," said Miss Gordon with a sneer. "Unfortunately, it's quite out Of date since Landor and Sven Hedin, and I have seen remaindered copies of it offered at two-and-sixpence in the booksellers' catalogues. Admitting that he was a very wonderful traveller, and that he wrote well, that doesn't give Lady Lindsay any right to hold herself so superior to all of us. lam sure that there are better families than hers in the Lake District. Why, here in Ambleside-- And after all, she is comparatively a newcomer amongst us, and so what right has she to make such a fuss about these advertisements? It seems to me that these Sarsaparilla people are entitled to advertise their wares in any way that seems to them most advantageous, and that if we, who have been born and bred here, can put up with it it is no business of hers. Of course," she added, with a titter, "I know that the stuff is advertised as supreme for the complexion,' and that she may take for a personal allusion." "Was it ever proved, by the way:' asked one of the ladies, "that Maud Lindsay did pull down that big board on the Waterhea,d-road ? I knosv that she was suspected of doing so." "No," said Miss Gordon; "aid it's a good thing for Miss Lindsay that it could not be proved. I hear that the agent wafi furious, and said that he would spare no expense to bring the guilt home to the offender. If it was Miss Lindsay, she has only cut off her nose to spite her face for now she has got a Sarsaparilla yacht at anchor outside her house—and a very hideous thing it is too, I must say." "It must be all the more annoying to the Lindsays," said another lady, "because they have got another paying guest—a Mr. Brown I believe his name is. A young fellow down from Oxford." "Ah, yes, I had forgotten about Lady Lindsay's paying guests," said Miss Gordon. "There's another reason why she should not put on such airs. When one turns one's home into a kind of boarding-house—" "Not a bad idea," said the lady who had mentioned Mr. Brown's name, "when you have grown-up daughters on your hands. Young men from Oxford are often susceptible, and there's no denying that Maud is a very pretty girl. A Tomboy, if you like, but decidedly attractive. I was quite surprised that it didn't come off with • Charles ~What's-his-name, the last paying guest.", "Sir Charles wanted money," said Miss Gordon, "and the Lindsays haven't any. I believe that the bulk of their income came out of Sir Harold's copyrights—royalties—what d' ye- call 'em ?—and now that Landor and Sven Hedin have spoilt the sale of that great work--" "Yes, I believe that Lady Lindsay has rather a hard time of it," said the lady of the house. "Well," concluded Miss Gordon, before intro- ducing a fresh piece of malevolent gossip, "I would give much to hear what Maud Lindsay has to say about the latest advertising move of her friends the Sedleys." Even the spiteful Miss Gordon would have been satisfied if at that very moment she could have been with the party on Crusoe's that lovely little spot at the head of Lake Windermere, which for some yeafs past had been the home of Lady Lindsay and her two daughters. For just then the Sarsaparilla yacht was being brought to anchor at her moorings, in full sight of Crusoe's Cave, as Lady Lindsay's little cottage was called. The boat was trimly built enough, but the manner in which she was painted, in Daring reds and blues, with the name of the specific displayed on sails and pennants, rendered her a very eyesore in the lovely scene. "They will never have the impudence to leave that wretched thing on the lake!" cried Maud Lindsay, springing from her seat with such impetuosity that she nearly upset the table at which, with her mother, sister' and guest, she was taking afternoon tea on the lawn, which sloped down to the waterside. "That they certainly will have," said the paying guest, a handsome young fellow. "I heard in town to-day that they have rented those moorings for the season. They mean to parade the lake all the summer, brass band and all, till not a soul in the district, not a tripper from anywhere, shall ignore the virtues of Sedley's Sarsaparilla." "We'll see about that!" cried Maud Lindsay, clenching her little fist and turning pale with

indignation. "By Jove! Miss Lindsay," cried the young -man, with a look of very evident admiration at the girl, "I wish I could paint your picture as -you stand there, defying the—the—well, the Sedleys. You look a very heroine, ready to do and die in a just cause." "And what cause could be juster," cried Maud Lindsay, laughing in spite of herself," than s revolt against such odious desecration of one of the prettiest scenes in England ? I love this place. It is to me something hallowed, something sacred. I will not stand this insult thrown in , the face of Nature. I—l—will—" "I do hope, dear," said Lady Lindsay, "that you will do nothing foolish. Remember what a terrible fuss was made about that hoarding on the Waterhead-road. Mr. Barton 9-poke of offering fifty pounds' reward for the detection of the person who hacked it down. And I believe it is only because Mr. Barton had a very shrewd suspicion who that person was that he did not proceed any further." "Very kind of Mr. Barton!' cried Maud. "He shews his consideration for that person's feelings by replacing that hoarding with something ten times worse—a million times worse!" "Mr. Barton ?" asked the young man. "Yes Mr. Brown," said Lady Lindsay. "He is the advertising agent for these Sedley people. He has a big office in Liverpool, I believe, and is a very rich man. It is he who is responsible for all these horrible—horrible—well, notions ' he calls them. Yes, that's it, advertising

- , notions . "He must be a very go-ahead man, who works well for his employers, said the young man. "I saw this Sarsaparilla stuff advertised all the Nvay up from ,Oxford. I declare I WS3 almost hypnotised into investing in a bottle, by the time I had reached Windermere. I must day, though," he added, "that it's a shame to put that thing there, just opposite your house, and I can quite understand Miss Lindsay's indignation, it entirely spoils the look out, and-- But here's Miss Lindsay with tears in her eyes!" "It was father's favourite spot," said the girl, brushing her hands over her eyes, "and---and- But I declare." she cried, with an entire change

===== 0002601_19070921_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
or tone and manner, "that horrible man is coming here! Look, mother, here is Mr. Barton rowing to the island." "Is that Mr. Barton ? " said the young man. "Yes," said the girl; "that short, red-haired fellow in the stern of the boat. Of all the impudence! I won't see him, mother. I know he wants to see me, but tell him that I'm ill, lying down, or anything. I won't see him ! " "I am afraid he has already seen you," said Lady Lindsay. "I think it would be wiser to remain, if you want to conciliate him." "Conciliate him!" cried Maud, with an expres- sive gesture. It was then, however, too late for her to escape. The boat had run alongside the little landing of Crusoe's Island, and the "short, red- haired man." who was not very short, and whose hair was blonde rather than ruddy, had briskly climbed ashore and was making his way, hat a hand. towards the party on the lawn. "Oh, good evening, Lady Lindsay," said the newcomer. "Please excuse my unceremonious call at this hour. But I feel I owe you some apology for—for—well, that ! " he cried, waving his hand towards the hideous little yacht at anchor. "It's not my fault. lam only acting under orders. Yes, under orders from New York. There was—ahem! some suspicion at headquarters as to well, as to that little occurrence on the Waterhead-road, and certain feelings were aroused, which I had great difficulty in pacifying. I ma •• say that it was entirely owing to my intervention "—this with a glance at Maud, who promptly turned her back upon ho....—"that certain proceedings were not insti- tuted." "Do you mean to say, Mr. Barton," cried Maud, swinging round upon him, "that that dis- gusting object is to remain there, outside our windows poisoning the view for us and spoiling all our pleasure in life ? " "Oh, come, Miss Lindsay," sad the advertis- ing agent, "isn't that a bit strong—' poisoning the view' ? Let me tell you, Miss Lindsay. that, far from poisoning anything, Sedley's Sarsa- parilla—" "Yes, we know all that," said Mr. Brown. "It's on all your posters. But do you mean to tell me, sir, that the Sedley Sarsaparilla Company of New York authorises you to employ advertis- ing notions' which give offence to people, very serious offence, as in this case?" "Not having the pleasure of your acquaintance, sir," said Mr. Barton, "I don't see what right you have either to interrupt me as you have done, or to ask me questions on business matters which concern me alone." The young man shrugged his shoulders arid turned away. "To return to what I was saying," said the agent. "That yacht is to lie there, where it is now located. lam sorry that that spot and no other should have been chosen, but it is none of my doing. And I hope," he added, raising his voice for the benefit of Maud, who had moved away, following Mr. Brown, "that no attempts will be made similar to what happened on the Waterhead-road. A prosecution for wilful damage would be the inevitable consequence without prejudice to an action in the civil court for damages. It is quite hopeless for any evil-minded person or persons to try and fight the Sedley Manufacturing Company. We have millions of dollars behind us, my lady—millions of dollars!" "Do you think he is telling the truth," said Mr. Brown to Maud, who had now strolled some way off, "when he says that it is not his fault ? " "Of course I don't!" cried Maud. "He is doing this merely to spite me. The horrid wretch wants me to marry him. I have refused him three times already without thanks, and this is his revenge." "Oh, that's it, is it ? " said the young man. "Well, under those circumstances I think I can authorise you to take any steps that—ahem! —you may decide upon to—to well—shall we say upset Mr. Barton's applecart ? " "Upset Mr. Barton's yacht would be more to the point," said Maud, laughing. "Very kind of you, to be sure, Mr. Brown, to authorise me in that lordly manner. I think if you were sincere in what you said to me this morning—wasn't it that you would do anything and everything to prove-well, you know the rest—that wretched thing would not be there to-morrow morning." "Ah! there you are asking me too much. You have heard what he says. I could not risk a suit for damages. I am not of age, and have no money beyond my allowance. I couldn't pay the damage, and the magistrates would send me to Kendal gaol." "Well, if I loved a girl," said Maud—"that is, if I were a man—l should be pleased and proud to--" "Bore a hole in Sedley's Sarsaparilla yacht and sink it in twenty fathoms of water. Well, I come of age to-morrow and shall then have a little money at my disposal, and—well, I'll think the matter over." "Well," said Maud, "whilst you are thinking it over, go and have a look in the tool- house." When two days later Lady Lindsay came down to breakfast she found her daughters and Mr. Brown standing by the window which overlooked the lake. "Now I declare," she cried, "that is nice of Mr. Barton! He has taken the horrid thing away. I did plead so hard with him the other day. Maud, you see that it is always better to act with diplomacy." "Or with an axe," said Maud. "Well,the yacht has gone, and that is the principal thing, is it not, Mr. Brown ?" "The outlook is certainly improved," said the young man; "but if there has been any work with an axe, there--" "Oh, that would be too dreadful !" cried Lady Lindsay. "Maud, why are you laughing? It cannot be possible. Why that would mean my ruin, if Mr. Barton were to find out--" "Yes," said Mr. Brown, "there's no doubt he'd be in no end of a stew about it." "But supposing I had done anything of the kind," said Maud. "I was fully authorised by Mr. Brown, was I not, and--" "Maud," cried Lady Lindsay, "you do not mean to say— Mr. Brown, what does she

? " - mean "I said to your daughter the day before yesterday, and again yesterday, that, as far as I was concerned, she wss quite at liberty to take any steps that shc? liked to put a stop to the nuisance of which you have all been complaining." "Well, Mr. Brown," said the old lady, "if you have been setting my foolish child on to do such an unheard-of act of folly, I hope you will be prepared to take the consequence upon your shoulders." "I have no dearer wish in this world," said Mr. Brown, "than to take the entire responsi- bility of Miss Maud Lindsay and of all her acts on my shoulders if you think that they are broad enough to bear them." "But, my dear sir," said the old lady, "I know so little of you. Have you the means to keep a wife And Maud, what does she say ? " "Maud says," said the girl, blushing, "that, though she is quite ready to take the responsi- bility of her own acts, she is not unwilling--" "To let Mr. Brown share them," added the young man, clasping her in his arms. "Lady Lindsay, I love your daughter with all my heart. shall be able to satisfy you as to my position and means. And now let us get to breakfast. I see storm and strife ahead in the shape of Mr. Barton, and we must keep our strength up to face the encounter." And not later than that evening the encounter had announced itself as a strenuous one. The news that Jane, the younger daughter, brought home from Ambleside was that there never had been such a "to-do." When it had been established that the yacht had been sunk at her moorings, Mr. Barton's rage had "been something awful." He had spent nobody knew how many pounds in cabling to America. He had conferred at length with the -police and a large order for posters beginning "Whereas," and offering a big reward, was being proceeded with at the printing works of the local newspaper. " said the young man, very quietly. As he spoke he rose and took a step towards the agent. "I don't think that the Sedley Company would back you up in such aggressive action; in fact, I am sure they would not. They may be makers of quack medicines, but there is, I believe, some gentle- manly feeling amongst them. But that is beside the question. I dispute your statement that you are empowered to act in their name. Your con- tract with the Sedley Manufacturing Company lapsed when the powers of the trustees by whom that concern was being run, also lapsed." "You seem to know a lot about my affairs, young fellow," said the agent. "It is quite true that my renewed contract has to be ratified by the new proprietors of the concern, but in the mean- while--" "In the meanwhile you had better get out of this house as quickly as you can. I warned you when you came in here not to raise your voice, and generally to try and conduct yourself like a gentleman. Your contract has not been renewed and never will be, and you may write a couple of thousand a-year off your income, Mr. Barton. May it serve you as a lesson to conduct your business in a more gentlemanly manner. The Sedley Company does not want bullies and black- mailers in its employment." "Bullies and blackmailers ? " cried the agent, foaming at. the mouth. "Yes. There are no other words with which to describe your conduct. You have been pester- in' this young lady with your unwelcome atten- tions, and because she sent you about your busi- ness, you revenged yourself on her by putting that hideous eyesore outside the house. It was a very contemptible trick, and contemptible tricks are prejudicial to business. So your contract will not be renewed." "And who are you, Mr. Brown, to talk to me like that ? " "I am sorry to say, ladies," said the young man, turning his smiling face towards Lady Linqay and her daughters, "that / am the Sedley Manufacturing Company." "Oh, you horrid man!" cried Maud. "You don't look like a company, I must say,* cried Jane. "Mr. Barton," continued Mr. Brown, holding out a letter which he had taken from his pocket- book, "do you recognise this writing ? " The agent snatched the paper from his hand, glanced at it, and cried: "Yes, I do. That letter's in my writing, appointing a meeting in London for to-morrow afternoon with Sir John Hauberk, of Christ Church College, Oxford, at the offices of the Sedley Company's London solicitors. And I'd like to know how this letter came into your hands, Mr. Brown ? " "It was forwarded to me from the House yesterday," said the young man. "Mr. Barton, may I introduce myself as the Sir John Hauberk in question." "But," cried Jane, "you said just now that you were a manufacturing company." "Well, I'm that too," said the young man; "hut only since yesterday, when I came of age. Behold in me, ladies, and Mr. Barton, the Sedley Sarsaparilla, that excellent specific for—well, for details see small bills. When Sedley,the inventor of this grand medicine, died he left his business to his widow, who a year or two later came to England, where she shortly afterwards married my father, Sir John Hauberk. After my mother's death the business was managed during my minority by trustees in America, and as a minor, of course I had no voice in the way it was con- ducted, and so I must disclaim all responsibility for the vulgar and offensive methods which were adopted to push the sales of the stuff. Things . will be altered now that I am master, and, as • you have seen, ,in getting rid of Mr. Barton. I

narre arrenarcuaten a szepar znat, arreation.

"That's all very fine,; said the agent. "But if you are Sir John Hauberk, what do you go masquerading as Mr. John Brown for? I shall want something more than your word before I am satisfied."

"You shall have every satisfaction," said the baronet. "We will keep that appointment to- morrow in town, Mr. Barton, at my solicitors' offices. Your remarks suggest to me that I owe some -explanation to these ladies about what you call masquerading. I won't give as a motive for hiding my identity that I wanted a quiet holiday down here, and that quiet and seclusion are re- fused to a man who is a baronet, and who through no fault of his own is afflicted with a huge fortune amassed in the way we know. Those are sentiments which you Mr. Barton, will hardly understand. Supposing I tell you that down at Oxford I had heard all about your pro- ceedings here, and that, having the interests of my property at heart, I came down quietly, under a name which would prevent your recog- nition of me, to satisfy myself whether the com- plaints against you were justified. I was more than satisfied long ago, but until yesterday, being stillia minor, I had no power to interfere. Nty first act on becoming yesterday the Sedley Sarsa- parilla Company was to authorise this young lady, my affianced wife, to destroy that floating nuisance. You understand, constable," he added, turning to the policeman, "that there can be no action for wilful damage taken on the informa- tion laid by that man. The yacht was mine, and mine to do what I choose with. He is merely a naid servant of mine, or rather was. for I have dispensed with his services, and so' constable, I your duties being ended, if you like to go into the kitchen Lady Lindsay's cook will, lam sure, be glad to offer you some refreshment. There is a fine round of -told beef, and the home- brewed- well, you will give me your ophaion of it. As to you. Mr. Barton. I shall be very glad to see you down to the landing. We will meet in town to-morrow."

"I don't need your company down to the landing," cried the agent, dashing his hat upon his head. "I —I despise you, Sir John—l heartily despise you. A pretty mess you'll make of the business, I can see." With these words he darted out of the room, tri,34 a minute later was seen rowing away in the nrection of Waterhead pier. "And now, Maud," said the baronet, laying his hands on the girl's shoulders and looking into her eyes with a smile, "do you think that you can forgive me for being a Sarsaparilla Company. You must remember, dear, that I only became one yesterday, and that to-morrow I hope to be one no longer. The trustees," he added, turning to Lady Lindsay, "have made a very large offer to take the whole concern over, and I had long decided to take it. I shall invest the money in land, and, though that won't pay a tenth part of the dividend that sarsaparilla pays, I'd rather get our income that way than from--" "Pains in Little Mary," said Jane. "You are quite right, little sister," said Sir John Hauberk. "I may call you that for I can see in Maud's eyes that she has forgiven me." • [THE END.]

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The Assistant Wok- and the Cricket

Match.

MR. JOLLEY CRITICISES THE ESTIMATES.

Lively Passages.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday;l4lr. W. Houghton presiding. There were also present :—Mrs. Simpson, Mrs. Wilkinson,Miss Broadbent, Rev. M.Ryan, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Billinge, W. Furness, W. C. Brooks, T. Ritchie, M. W. Seton, G. Mather, W. Flannagan, J. Cook, J. Ball, A. Brooks, R. Boltcy, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Cottage Homes. THE WORKHOUSE AN "INFECTED AREA." At a meeting of the House Committee it was reported. that the Board of Agriculture had declared the Work- house premises to be an infected area for three months. Mr. MATHER said this appeared to be a formidable declaration,but in his opinion there had never been any swine fever on the premises. To make the house an infected area would do neither good nor harm, as they would not be purchasing any more pigs for three months. The minutes of the Committee were approved. , THE ASSISTANT CLERK AND THE CRICKET M TCH. COMMITTEE'S DIGNITY OFFENDED. The minutes of the Padgate Cottage Homes Corn- mittee included the following :—" The attention of the Committee having been called to an advertisement purporting to be issued by the Assistant Clerk to the Guardians relative to a cricket match, the proceeds of which are stated to be for the purchase of prizes for the children of the Cottage Homes, expresses its strong disapproval of the way in which the Assistant Clerk appears to have acted in the matter." The CLERK said it was a somewhat unusual thing, unless the officer affected had been asked to explain, to make a record of that kind in a book which went before the public. He only spoke of the practice upon that point. His son had only human nature, like other people and he might, as many others did, have corn-' nutted indiscretion. It was a little hard upon an officer that without asking him for a personal explana- tion and without detailing the nature of his wrong, to pass such a resolution. Whatever it might be, be (Mr. Sutton) did not know. But this he did know—that his ' son had been very enthusiastic in the work, and how-, ever faulty he might have been in his procedure, his heart had been in it. He had wished to carry out what had occurred previously. There had been matches of that kind before, in which Mr. Awbery took a valuable part, to his credit. He (Mr. Sutton) supposed that being influenced by the memory , of what had occurred before, his son had acted in that ' matter, without, having, as he (Mr. Sutton) admitted he ought to have done, consulted the Committee and Guardians and obtaining their approval of the pro- cedure. He asked the Chairman of the Committee to make known what the indiscretion was, for these were grave words to be recorded without specifying what the sin was. Mr. MATHER, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said the indiscretion the Assistant Clerk had committed was to issue the advertisement to the public and arrange the match without the knowledge or _consent of the Guardians. The committee felt he ought to have let them know what was going on. The team included officials belonging to the institution who would have to get leave of absence. The CLERK agreed that the Guardians should have been consulted. ,The CHAIRMAN suggested that the matter should be reconsidered by the committee at their next meeting and % mollifying recolution passed. The minutes were seconded by Mr. Jolley and approved.

CROSFIELDS' BOWLING CLUB.

Distribution of Prizes.

The Workmen's Dining Hall at Messrs. Crosfields' I was the scene of a very interestiny, little function on t Monday evening last when the prizes were distributed by Miss Markel to the successful competitors in the annual bowling main held on Saturday last at the Recreation Ground. Mr. JOHN WILLETT who presided congratulated his fellow-members on the success of the main. For some- time the ancient pastime had languished amongst them and the annual main once the greatest sporting event of the works year had fallen into neglect but the open- ing of the Recreation Ground there had been a most gratyying revival culminating in Saturday's competition the interest in which had evidently not yet subsided as was evidenced by the large assembly he saw before him. The presence of Dr. and Miss Markel moved him to say how very fortunate they were in having a body of directors who took a sincere interest iu their health and happiness. He bad been under Crosfields' practic- ally all his life and he ventured to say that there was not a single firm in the town that approached them in consideration arid practical provision for the welfare of their employees. (Applause). The prizes having been distributed by Miss Markel, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded on the motion of Mr. TOMKINSON, seconded by Mr. MELLOR. Dr. MARKEL, yielding relucantly to the general call for a speech, briefly thanked the meeting on behalf of his daughter for the cordial way in Which it had supported her in the performance of this function which he could assure them had been a very great pleasure to her. He complimented them on the success of the main. He had not, unfortunately, the pleasure of witnessing it but judging from the number and handsome appearance of the prizes it must have been a successful Main indeed. As for the firm, he could only say that any efforts his co-directors and himself had. made for the welfare of their employees were, they felt, quite justified by the good feeling which happily existed in every depart- ment, and which was in such happy contrast to the unrest they saw in some other industries. Further these efforts would be continued at. any sacrifice. (Applause.) They were as his hearers knew passing through a very difficult crisis in the trade which called for vigilance and considerable economy but of this they might be certain that the old firm had measured up the situation and settled its policy accordingly and would emerge from the struggle with flying colours. (Applause.) Mr. F. J. LORD having also said a few words the meeting terminated. The principal winners were :-1, C. Millington; 2, W. Pritchard ; 3, J. Fairhurst; 4 J. !Mellon 5, 3. Hill ; 6, J. Jefferson ; 7, R. Welsby, 8, J. Davies.

FAMILY NEGLECT.

Warrington Fathers Sent to Gaol.

Two cases of family neglect were heard at the Warrington Police Court on Saturday. Samuel Beckett, 9, Crossley-street, was charged with neglecting to main- tain two children, whereby they had become chargeable to the Union, and also with having been previously convicted of a similar offence.

Mr. Shearer, relieving officer. stated that prisoner was sentenced to two months in October last for neglecting his children. One of them had been in the Workhouse for eight years. Prisoner Could earn 235. per week if he would work. His house was in a dread- full condition through neglect.

Prisoner said he had not sufficient work to bring his children out of the Union.

The Chief Constable said there were 39 convictions against prisoner and the magistrates passed sentence of two months' imprisonment. A COSTLY FAMILY. Robert Burke, 122, Forster-street, was charged with neglecting his four childrea in a similar manner.

Mr. Sharer said the children had beenja the Union nearly four years. They had cost the Guardians £l5O to maintain, and prisoner had not paid a penny. Prisoner could earn good wages when he worked. The Chief Constable pointed out that prisoner had six months in 1903 for neglecting his children. Mr. Shearer added that prisoner had said he would never pay. _ _ . _ _

. . The Bench sentenced prisoner to a month's imprison- ment.

AN EAPTY iOUSE.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Thomas Hudson, School-brow; pleaded guilty to failing to maintain his t4o children, whereby they had become chargeable to the Union.

Mr. Shearer said prisoner's children became charge- able on September 11, 1906, and they had been in the Workhouse ever since. He had done nothing whatever to maintain them. Witness had been to prisoner's house and found there was nothing in it. Prisoner said be was willing to keep his children. The Bench sent him to gaol for 14 days.

STRANGE POLICE ERROR.

Prosecutions against two Damen cotton manufactur ing firms for exceeding the speed limit with moto- lorries had a singular ending at Bolton on Thursday Messrs. T. and R. Eccles had been fined £5 fora similar offence at the same place, and notice of appeal was given. Subsequently the Chief Constable of the county communicated with the appellants' solicitors, informing him it had been ascertained that the police had made a mistake in the measurements, and under those circum- stances he proposed that the conviction should not be recorded. The prosecutions in the other cases were formally withdrawn.

To those who do not under- METHODIST stand Nonconformity the RE-UNION. union of three of the Methodist denominations which has just taken place in London must be somewhat bewildering, if they take any notice of it at all, as they certainly ought to do should they wish to understand what is going on around them and to measure the relative value of existing religious forces. To those who have not given any of their attention to Methodist organisation, the joining together of what they consider to be three forms of religion, must necessarily be of the nature of the inexplicable. The explanation is easy enough, nevertheless. It has not been neces- sary to make any sacrifice of doctrine on either side. The three bodies which have now joined them- selves together did not teach different things. There has been no time when their preachers could not exchange pulpits, when the members of one Church could not worship with perfect satis- faction in another, or when there was any vital or even clearly perceptible doctrinal differences between the Bible Christians,the United Methodist Free Church, and the Methodist New Connexion. They were divided merely on matters of Church government, not by contending dogmas. And that is what is meant, indeed, by most of our divisions into sects. Many of the bodies which are vulgarly supposed to represent different " religions " are, in fact, in perfect doctrinal agreement.

_ For our own part, we see no harm from the point of view of religious effectiveness in such divisions as those which have been brought to an end in Wesley's Chapel in London. They tend to produce individuality, strength of character,and self-reliance. The struggle for existence is too keen to permit of the development of priestly characteristics among the ministery. There is not that separation between ministers and people which is so generally brought about by comfort- able salaries, big churches, and irremovability. On the other hand, division among Churches that might as well be allied often results in a great waste of effort and a quite unneces- sary expense. This is not the case to the same extent in large centres of population as in the smaller towns and the villages. Here, where the population provides a congregation probably ~ not more than sufficient for one good-sized Methodist chapel, there may be three or four, each. sparsely attended. The obliteration of this state of things will probably be one of the great advan- tages of the new union. The different congrega- tions will join their efforts, and, in Cobbett's , I phrase, they will " club their twopences." I Deeply interesting to old Methodists, deeply affecting, indeed, must have been the re-union 1 gatherings. There were men on the platform who had been through stirring times that took place many years before most of us were born. They 1 belong to the time of 1 Old, remembered, far-off things, 1 And battles long ago. There was the Rey. J. SWA NN WITHINGTON, for instance. He was already a famous preacher when that earlier union took place which brought the United Methodist Free Church into existence. He was the colleague of GRIFFTHS, EVERETT, and DUNN, those three stout-hearted men who left the Wesleyan Methodists because they could not set up the democratic standard within that body. The Rev. EDWARD BOADEN is another link with the past. He is now elected first President of the newly- united Churches; but he wass a leading man at the Conference at which RICHARD CHEW was elected President of the United Methodist Free Church at Manchester. That must be nearly forty years ago. Both EVERETT and GRIFFITHS were then living:and each preached a Conference sermon, GRIFFITHS holding his audience spellbound for an hour and twenty minutes, and then making a phenomenal collection. There seemed to be more life, and more abundant life, in the Methodism of those days than there is in that of our own. But we may be wrong, of course. Allowance must be made for the glamour of the retrospect. And certainly the union which has taken place in London should tend to the restoration of the vigour of the Methodist sects should any of it prove to have been lost.

The Education Committee

THE FEEDING OF on Monday postponed the SCHOOL CHILDREN, question of adopting the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1906. This is a permissive measure, giving power to Education Authorities to provide, if they thick fit, free meals to elementary school children who are in the habit of attending school while insufficiently fed. A report has been pre- pared by the Education Authorities in Warrington and when this has been considered the question will come up again. We see no objection to the adoption of the Act, but the greatest caution will require to be exercised before it is put in force. The fact that a certain number of children come to school unfed and are entitled to free meals from the muni- cipality is not the height and width of the question

by any means. Interference with parental respon- sibility comes in immediately the Corporation begins to feed the children of the poor. A too generous provision of free meals wont.' soon do vastly more harm than good. It has to be dis- covered which children are hungry through poverty and which through neglect. In the latter cases pressure will have to be put on the parents to make them realise their responsibilities. Then there are the agencies for distribution and the committee of control to be considered. All these things need to occupy the mind of the ,educationist very seriously before the Act is made operative. We believe the problem is not one of great magnitude in Warrington for out of some 14,000 scholars it is estimated that there are less than 200 children attending school insufficiently fed; these are classified as 150 cases of poverty and 40 of neglect. The actual feeding of these poor mites should not be difficult, and will doubtless ere long be undertaken.

A perusal of the calendar " MODERN " for the new session of EDUCATION. the London School of Economics is an excellent illustration of the " modern " as distinguished from the " classical " trend in higher education. The ideal in v:ew is to arrange a curriculum which will supplement the student's daily work in the office or workshop, so that the two taken together shall constitute his education. For example one whose daily work is connected with railways may attend lectures on Railway Economics, the Law of Traffic Relations, Railway Operat- ing, and so on, so that he may be enabled to take a more intelligent interest in his work. Other subjects included under the wide heading of "Economics and Political Science" are Banking and Librarianship, tne other depart- ments of London University dealing in a similar way with the work of many other trades. It is interesting to note that the somewhat indefinite subject of Sociology is so arranged as to be of interest to the following list of people: Borough Councillors and Poor Law Guardians, com- mittee men of philanthropic institutions and officers of benevolent societies, district visitors, factory inspectors, trade-union officials, and workers in social settlements. This is the subject, be it remembered, the very existence of which is not recognised by " classical " Universities because of the hybrid origin of its name. London's pre- eminent suitability as a place of study is illustrated by the fact that the School of Economics can direct its students to the Record Office for medians' history, and to the British Museum and the libraries of the Government Depattments for modern research. Even Oxford, with its Bodleian, could not surpass these collections—indeed, it could not equal them.

By Royal Proclamation ' THE DOMINION OF New Zealand is no longer NEW ZEALAND. a colony but a dominion. Did Tasnian, when he explored the land in 1612, with the Dutch East India Company waiting for his report, have any vision of the pride of name which took the type clean across the page of the last issue of the ordinarily double-columned London official Gazette? Of course, New Zealand is little better for the title. It is no bigger than it was in 1777, when Captain Cook went there, two years before the South Sea islanders sharpened their 'knives for him at Hawaii ; nor has it reached a European population of a million, unless it has made up the lee-way of 10,000 since last year's census. It is a trifle smaller than Great Britain and Ireland, while its public revenue last year was 7i millions. Still, it is a great country, and Sir Joseph Ward has.a right to be proud of it. He saw that it took its right place in the Parliament of Empire this summer, and nobody can forget, in these days when the mother, with a countenance florid and slightly anxious, is trying to keep pace with her modern daughter, that New Zealand sets us an example in those two cardinal developments of twentieth century Liberalism, women's suffrage and old-age pensions. There women vote for Parliament, and there women as well as men receive an £lB a-year pension when they reach sixty-five— provided always that the husband has not been basely deserted in the mean- time. The number of pensions in force two years ago was 11,700, representing 35 per cent of the Iresidents of New Zealand over the age of sixty- five, and of this number 632 were Maoris.

The threat of a railway THE RALLWAY strike has had the effect of PROBLEM. directing the attention of the public more seriously than before to the question of the proper adoainis tration of the system of communication through - out the country in the future. If the present trouble in the railway world were to issue in a cessation of work by even a section of the railway employees the effect upon trade would be dis astrous. The great companies admit that the public would suffer enormous inconvenience, and the estimate of loss involved upon the trading community is almost incalculable. In such circumstances it is not strange that serious-minded people should devote themselves to an exanaina tion of the position which other great commercia countries have found themselves forced to adopt. England is particularly fortunate in her railways, taking them in comparison with other countries. She has fine systems linking up the great centres of industry, managed with enterprise and upon enlightened lines. Her services are the safest in the world, and the travelled man knows the value of the care devoted to securing efficiency, and protection for passengers and goods. In a time like the present, when the tendency in many quarters is to regard railway directors as blind and obstinate opponents to pro- gress, these things should be recognised. Private enterprize has done great things for commanica- tion in England; but, as Lord BRASSY pointed out at Liverpool, there are flaws. Carrying rates are high—too high for the industrial welfare of the country in many instances. That is a fault partly due to the comparatively short distances for which goods have to be conveyed and partly to the excessive competition which has "gone on. Money has been wasted upon duplicate lines, and the competition of these lines has been bad both for the railway worker and for the com- panies themselves. All these things and many others, which became more and more apparent at each half-yearly gathering of directors and share- holders incline Lord BEASSEY to the belief that a vital change in the policy of the country towards railways may soon be regarded as a question of practical politics. Nationalisation has taken place in Germany—where its success has been great —in Russia, in Belgium, and in Italy. Why not here? The answer, of course, is that, as things are at present, there is no defiaite call for change, and the cost would therefore scarcely be justified. But if the country were plunged into a railway war of any prolongation the situation would be different, and the country , might be prepared to face the financial sacrifice involved to secure itself in the future from the disaster of the past. If the operation of private , enterprise in English railways had been less bene- ficial and proficient, the demand might be more easily imperative; but as matters stand, whatever the theoretic value of the argument that railways should be run with a single eye to the public service, the practical side of tho question gives a balance on the side of continuing the present status which will require a deal of shifting.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

It is proclaimed in canon 74 that the clergy shall not wear "any light-coloured stockings." I hope no parson will attempt to escape by such a miserable subterfuge as saying that this does not apply to socks. So if the reader sees any lawn-tennis or croquet playing curate with gaudy socks, clocked and decorated with un-comely and vain devices, he may say "Reverend Sir—what price =non 74 ? "—S. L. Hughes in "London Opinion." ___ .

An elderly farmer in the Claro district of Yorkshire was giving his opinion of modern journalism the other day. "No wonder," said he, "God Awmighty Isends us dull seasons. If there's a bit o' fine weather these editors and nevvspappers are puffin' and blowin' and sayin" Oh deary me, whatever shall we do if it's agoin' to be so warm ? ' And there's people writin' to t'pappers and o' mak of worrittin' goin' on, 'ut it's no wonder God Awmighty doesn't send us summers like there used to be when ah were a lad." * In the barn of a moorland farm near Glossop there is still to be seen a survival of the old industries which have mostly been stamped out by machinery. It is a sand-mill of very primitive form, worked by horse-power. It consists of a stone wheel, hooped with iron, running on its rim on a hard stone floor, and closely resembles the cider and wine presses that are commonly found in out-of-the-way villages both in France and Germany, though of course it is much bigger. The soft sandstone which is used fgr grind- ing is quarried from a neighbouring pit.

Presentations have been made this week to the Notts cricket professionals in recognition of the team's championship victory this season. Over £BOO was publicly subscribed, and of this sum, Hallam and Wass received £lOO each, Iremonger, J. G-unn, G. Gunn, Gates, Hardstaff, and Payton £9O each, and Alletson £55. Each amateur and professional also received a cigarette case given by Sir Francis Ley, the President of the Club, while the captain, Mr. A. 0. Jones, was presented with a massive silver rose- bowl. There are worse livelihoods than playing cricket for the man with any ability in the field.

o * The Education Jommittee has decided to sacrifice some slight principle on the score of generosity and pay the three pupil teachers the £7 which hal been deducted because they had lost the Government grant through failure to find admission to a training college. We are glad that Mr. Plinston's amendment has been carried. The School Management Committee was doubtless within its rights, but the teachers suffered a distinct hardship and had done nothing personally to forfeit £7 each. We can understand the reluctance to establish a precedent, but even a precedent is not binding and in this case it was justified. *

-Seeing that the Guardians and officials of the Warrington Union won their cricket match on Thurs- day, and will also be able to show a cash balance, perhaps the Board will be inclined to forgive the Assistant Clerk for letting his zeal outrun his discretion. Cricket matches are important things, and the season is getting late, and it might have caused considerable delay to have consulted the Board officially. They ought to have been consulted, of course, but we hope the mollifying resolution will be forthcoming at the next meeting. Mr. Sutton's enthusiasm was in a good cause.

* * * The Lincolnshire feat of transforming wheat into bread in four hours the other day is not in any sense a "record." It has been beaten at least twice within the last few years. On Whitehouse Common, near Erdington, in Warwickshire, the same feat was accomplished in three hours and a half about six years ago. But this was a dilatory performance com- pared with that which took place shortly afterwards on Mr. Taylor's farm at Blockley, in Worcestershire. Reaping began on a field of wheat at nine o'clock in the morning. Some portion of this was carried to the farm, threshed, and winnowed. It was then taken to the mills, cleaned, ground, and dressed. The flour was then hurried to the bakehouse, made into bread, baked, and some of it taken to the reapers and eaten in just sixty minutes after it had fallen. • * You must see life by the light of your own lamp. * * There were some lively p*assages at the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wednesday. ‘Mr. A. J. Jolley offered sundry criticisms of the 'estimates and even went so far as to move the levying of a Gd. rate instead of the 7d. recommended by the Clerk. Mr. Sutton warmly resented the proposal and advised the Board that if they fixed the reduced rate they would be obliged to make another levy to meet the year's expenses. No seconder of Mr. Jolley's amendment was forthcoming and the estimates as presented by the Clerk were approved. It was regrettable that so much heat was generated and that Mr. Sutton took the matter so- greally to heart as to express regret that he had not resigned before the meeting. We believe Mr. Jolley was within his rights in offering any comments he considered necessary, as this is the only opportunity afforded to the members to express themselves, but, as the Chairman hinted, he was inclined to pursue the matter too persistently. The Clerk assured the Board that the estimates were based on the most accurate information at his disposal and even if the 7d. call was found to produce too much, the money would be avail- able for the next half-year.

* • Some striking figures with regard to the smoke nuisance and its prevention were given in a paper read at the Sanitary Inspector's Conference at Llandudno on Wednesday by Mr. W. Nicholson, of Sheffield. He estimated that there was in the atmosphere over these islands daily thousands of tons of coal in the form of vapour, and of the 150,000,000 tons of c3al consumed annually in this country a third might be saved if a sensible and scientific system were adopted in the burning of the fuel. Owing to the crusade of the Smoke Abatement Association, founded in Sheffield due to the advocacy of the late Sir W. C. Leng, that city which was formerly so much afflicted with the smoke nuisance, had, owing to the adoption of drastic measures by the Corporation, so improved that last year it had 1,437 hours of sunshine, or 368 more than Manchester, 367 more than Bradford. 265 more than Glasgow, 189 more than Leeds, 186 more than Birmingham, 158 more than Edinburgh, 53 more than York, and 53 more than Bettws-y-Coed. He reoommended that the law, which now only prohibited " black smoke," should be made to apply also to every kind of visible smoke emitted from chimneys, and that domestic fires should be included, inasmuch as the smoke from dwelling-houses was due to defect in combustion which might be overcome by the provision of proper grates and by the proper management of the fires.

* * * The Act to permit the release on probation of offenders in certain cases is one of great significance. The special clause with regard to the absention from intoxicating liquor gives the magistrates a splendid opportunity of reforming the drunkard who comes before the Court charged with a minor offence. Magistrates now have the power to say to an offender that, to obtain the merciful consideration of the Court, he may purge his offence and avoid the stain and Ignominy of a conviction, provided he will promise not to drink for a prescribed period. Failure to keep such promise renders him liable to be arrested, con. - victed and punished for his original offence. This !S practically the plan which Judge Pollard initiated in his Court at St. Louis, and by which, after a four years' trial, he has saved hundreds of drunkards; 98 per cent keeping the pledge. To quote Judge Pollard the law became of more majesty when it saved, when it lifted up, and made a sober and better citizen than when it punished a man." Penalties and imprisonment have little effect on the drunkard. But the probationary period may help him to withstand temptation and become a useful citizen. Further information and pledge forms for use in Courts mai be obtained from Mr. Walter East, Marian House Leicester.

Miss Gladys Wilkinson in a few well- chosen words made the presentation on behalf of the firm, and hoped Miss Cummings would enjoy good health in her new sphere of life, and assured her that she went with the very best wishes of the firm. Miss Cummings suitably thanked the donors, and said the valuable presents would be looked upon by her in the far distant land with the pleasantest recollections and she hoped the wishes of her companions would he fulfilled. Shorthand Writers' Association, Excellent arrangements have been made for the ensuing session of this Association's work, which will be the second year of its existence. In addition to speed practice at varying rates from 70 words a minute upwards, and the elucidation of awkward and difficult outlines and other phonographic obscurities, as well as an examination in the "winged art," special attention has been directed to the literary half of the programme, which is a prominent feature of the Society. The papers and lectures comprise com- mercial, literary and scientific subjects of a high order, the list of lecturers including such well-known local names as the Rev. Canon S ;evenson, M.A., Mr. C. Madeley, Mr. E. S. A. Robson, 1N1.5.C., Mr. F. M. Darby, Mils. Bac., Mr. H. 11. Ashton, M.P.S., Mr. G. J. Brown, F.1.P.5., Mr. J. Watson. F.R.A.S., M I M.E., and several others. Nor has the social element been overlooked, two convivial gatherings being announced to take place during the session. A slight alteration has been made in the rules as to membership this year. Full members will now need to be capable of writing shorthand at the rate of at least 70 words a minute, but persons interested in the literary part of the programme will be eligible for admission as Associates, at a reduced subscription. The C immittee hope that their efforts will be appre- ciated by shorthand writers, and others interested, throughout the town, in a practical way, namely, by their joining the Association, and embracing and fully utilising the opportunities now offered to them, at a very moderate charge, of cultivating the mind and gaining knowledge from the lectures and papers which will be useful to them not only in the world of com- merce but in that greater world outside. The opening meeting for the session is fixed for Wednesday next, the 25th inst., at the Winmarleigh Caf6, Sankey- street, at 7.30 p.m., when speed practice will be followed by the reading of a paper entitled " Thomas Carlyle." Intending members are requested to attend this meeting for the purpose of enrolment. Any other information in reference to the Association may be obtained on application being made to the Hon. Secretary Mr. J. Marsh, 15 Rolleston-street, Warring- ton.

NATIONAL SERVICE LEAGUE. -- Fourth Annual Report.

The fourth annual report of the Warrington branch of the National Service League has just been issued. The balance sheet shows an income of £94 its. Gd. and an expenditure of £ll2 3s. 6d., deficit £l7 12s. ; the debit balance is accounted for, says the report, by the dona- tion to the Lancashire Committee (L 10 The increase in numbers continues to be very satis- factory. The thanks of the Committee are due to those honorary helpers whose efforts have largely been responsible for this gratifying addition to the strength of the Branch. In this connection they cannot forbear to make special reference to the excellent work done by Mr. Adams, of Messrs. Monks Hall and Co. The members of this firm alone are now 181. After reviewing the meetings which have been held during the year, the report goes on to say that shooting has become deservedly popular amongst the members of the Branch, and the thanks of every member are due to our President for providing Warrington with a range, as well as for the personal encouragement he has in other ways extended to rifle shooting. It is essential, however,that we should not lose sight cf the only practical way of achieving a real reform, one which will at one and the same time bring health and vigour to our city youth, and render this country immune from attack. If in Warrington, for example, we could prove that, let us say, three quarters of the voters were in favour of our proposals, there is little doubt that both candidates would speak out in favour of the League. This then is what we have got to strive for before the next election. We say the next election advisedly, for if we put off the day of preparation too long we may arrive too late. It is encouraging to hear on all hands that nothing has helped forward our cause more than Nfr. Haldane's Bill. Especially is this the case in Volunteer circles. Mr. Haldane has very rightly tried to improve the efficiency of the Volunteers by more stringent regulations. A private must in future serve for a period of four years ; he must go to camp at least for one week ; he must pay a fine if he wishes to leave before his period of service is up. These regulations are, we repeat in the right direction, because they make for efficiency But what do they show ? Why, the inherent injustice of expecting a few to do the duty of the many ; of increasing the load on the willing horse. We have no doubt, but that in a very short time Mr. Haldane's measure will prove the impossibility of inducing the patriotic few to undertake these additional burdens. Furthermore, the War Minister's doctrine that the really serious training can be given after war breaks out will itself make employers of labour think twice before employing men who have this liability hanging over their heads. It is a very different matter when the law is the same for all, especially as in this case, the serious training would be done while the man is young, at the age of IR or 19, when he can best be spared.

There are five aldermen who retire in November and rumour is already busy with their's and other names. Alderman Francomb is said to be unlikely to seek re-election, and the others who retire are Aldermen Smethurst, Bolton, Evans and Forshavv. The names of Councillors Evans, Fulton Smith, Burton and Ellison had been mentioned as possible candidates for any vacancies. Given a contest and there is likely to be some interesting voting on any of these names.

Holy Trinity Vicarage, 18th September, 1907. P.S.—The November elections are coining on and it will be just as well for the ratepayers to bear in mind not only the burden of taxation, which is bad enough, but also the fact that there are just now those in authority who are openly attacking the very founda- tions of liberty in the land.

The Board understood that it was the practice of some dealers though unaware of the diseased condition of the animals coming into their hands so to dispose of their carcases that there is risk that they will be used as food for the poorer classes in London and other large towns. TOWN COUNCIL. The November Changes.

A caucus meeting of the Town Council will be held on Monday evening to consider the election of Mayor for the coining year. Already people are beginning to talk of the November Elections, although the political parties have not yet Viken any steps to select candidates. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

Opening Next Monday.

Monday evening will witness the opening of the Palace and Hippodrome which has been erected at the junction of Barbauld-street and Friars' Gate, and we believe the verdict of Warrington theatre-goers I will be that the building is a great acquisition to the 1 town. The work which has proceeded with such remarkable rapidity is now almost completed, but yesterday the theatre still presented a scene of activity, workmen being engaged in putting the finishing touches to some of the embellishments which add a great deal to the general effect. The beautiful scenery was being placed in readiness for the opening performance, and it required no stretch of the imagination to realise that the appearance of the stage will be most pleasing. The theatre is handsome and commodious and everything possible is done for thc comfort of the audience. The seating arrange- ments are perfect and an uninterrupted view of the stage is obtained from every part of the house. The decorations are in the best of taste, the domed ceiling being particularly striking. For the opening week a splendid programme has been arranged. The chief attraction will be the musical farcical burletta entitled "High Jinks " or "Fun on the Sands" which will be presented by John Tiller's Company of forty performers. This is one of the smartest things of the kind on the variety stage, and its performance will occupy about forty-five minutes. It tells of the adventures which which befel Mr. George Desmond Done on h;,s little holiday and is certain to prove highly glansing. There are three scenes—On the sands; Corridor in the Casino; and The Casino. The Tiller troupe will appear as Maisie, Daisy,Minnie and Winnie, members of the Purity Brigade, and the company all round is an excellent one. The remainder of the programme will present many novel features. Kurkamp, the famous singing conductor, and his harpist, Chapman, will appear in a unique turn. Knrkamp possesses a wonderful voice and he not only sings but con- ducts the orchestra, while Chapina,n is an expert on the harp. The Sisters Reno, expert dancers, will provide a popular item, and Tennyson and Wallis, comedians, have the reputation of being well to the fore in their particular line. Some exceptionally fine animated pictures will be exhibited by Raymond's bio-tableaux. There will be two houses nightly—at 7 and 9—and the prices will suit all classes. They are :—Gallery 3d, early doors 4d. ; , pit (covering the whole of the ground floor) 6d. early I doors 9d. ; circle is., early doors is. 3d.

WHITE CITY, MANCHESTER.

-- - With a list of attractions covering almost every field of entertainment, the White City is a feature no one can afford to ignore. At no place in Great Britain has the field of amusement been more thoroughly covered, and it gives the place a distinct individuality, it is in a class entirely by itself, it has met with public fever ever since its opening. And now another new and distinct feature will be added. The Management have invited competition for a 9 stone Wrestling Championship Match,which will commence on Monday, October 7th, and continue daily. The first prize being a Championship Belt which becomes the absolute property of the winners. Other cash prizes are offered to other competitors. The competition will be conducted under the recognised rules of catch-as- catch-can style of wrestling in Lancashire. The interest already manifested in this competition is great, as most of all of the well-known wrestlers in this style have entered. At least two competitions will be given each evening in an especially constructed arena. To strengthen the artistic programme of the Rousey Concerts given under the direction of Herr Ludwig Blattner the well-known Irnpressario of Hamburg, Germany, which will be held in the maanificent ball- room at the White City on Friday afternoon and even- ing, September 20th, when Josef Rousey the "Gipsy Violinist" will make his first appearance since havir been commanded by H.M. The King, the manageny has engaged Signor Carlo Pollono, the popular ten' the Royal Opera House, Turin, Italy, who wi' make his first bow before a North of England r
