In connection with the Risley Independent Methodist Church, a grand bazaar was opened on Thursday afternoon by Mrs. Joseph Mason of Fearnhead, M. Adam Horrocks presiding. There was a good attend- ance at the opening proceedings including Councillor Barton of Warrington, and other well- known gentlemen. The bazaar is being held at Fearnhead in Mr. Joseph Mason's warehouse which has been very prettily decorated both outside and inside, flags and festoons being extensively used and stretching right across the highway. In addition fairy lights are hung upon the warehouse, making a very pretty effect at night. The ladies of the district patronised the stalls very freely during the course of the day At the end of the first day's proceedings 'it was stated that, including donations from Mr. Horrocks, Mr. Mason, Mr. Smith (who has recently cone t America) and others, the takings amounted' to £133. The object of the bazaar is for the erection of a new church.

Wesleyan Quarterly Meeting. The quarterly meetiniof the Warrington Wesleyan Circuit was held at Padgate on Thursday, when there was an unusually large number of representatives from the various places. Tea was provided in the schoolroom, and the business meeting held immedi- ately afterwards, the Rev. Sidney Pitt presiding. A most hearty welcome was accorded to the Rev. Thos. Miller, recently appointed as successor to the Rev. J. Manning. The numerical returns showed that there were 912 members in the circuit. The financial statement of the stewards, Mr. John Upson and Mr. R. H Meldrurn, was regarded as satisfactory, for whilst the ministerial change had necessitated increased expenditure, yet the ordinary income quite meets the ordinary requirements. After a brief conversation on the subject of the Class Meeting, it was decided to hold a convention of leaders at Bold- street on October 27th, with a view to the more efficient conducting of the classes. Foreign Mission affairs came under review, and the hope was expressed that the meetings to be held in the Autumn, for which arrangements have been 'made, would result in a further increase of income. Last year there was a satisfactory increase of over .00. Mr. W. Silcock was re-appointed missionary treasurer, and the Rev. Thomas Miller and Messrs. T. G. Withinshaw and G. Weaver, jun.. elected secretaries, with Mr. E. J. Mitchell as magazine secretary. Mr. J. E. Richardson was re-appointed treasurer of the Superannuated Ministers Fund. Attention was called by the Rev. Sidney Pitt to the claims of the fund, and he reported additional subscribers. The meeang throughout was characterisea by a spirit of groat hopefulness in view of the work of the New Ccanexional year. " HERALD " KITCHEN HINGES and " Peveril " Fire Grates are unsurpassed. Inspection invited. —J. S. GARLAND, 51, 55, Bridge-street, Warrington. —Advt. Presentation of Emblem of Merit. The quarterly meeting of the " Olive Branch Lodge" Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds, was held at the Rose and Crown Inn, Bridge-street, on Saturday last. The Lodge balance sheet was read by the Secretary (J. Haley, P.P.C.S.) and showed a net gain for the quarter of £7 18s. 2d.' and from the cor- responding quarter of last year of 07 2s. 3d., which was considered good for a small lodge of 27 members. —At the close of the ordinary business, the presenta- tion of an emblem of merit was made to Past Master James Denton. The emblem, which was handsomely framed in dark solid oak, was presented by the District Chief (Bro. Peter L. Edgar) who spoke of the sacrifice of the many pleasures of home and social life for the welfare and stability of these institutions of thrift, and complimented the recipient on his long connection of usefulness with the Lodge.—Bro. J. Baskerville, P.P.C.S., referred in warm terms to the worthy compliment paid to a worthy brother.—The Deputy Chief (Bro. Gilhooley) spoke in warm terms of appreciation of Bro. Denton's loyalty to duty.— Br.'. William Miles spoke of the recipient's long con- nection with the Lodge, and his retention of harness after many years of service.—After a few words from the Secretary, Bro. Denton very feelingly returned thanks for the many kind things said about him, and promised to still go on in the same old path of duty.— The emblem was greatly admired by many who cattle to view it. EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

POACHING.

At the Newton Petty Sessions last week Charles Collier was charged with poaching on land belonging to Lord Newton on September 3rd. Mr. Arthur Browne prosecuted for Lord Newton.—William Middleton, keeper, deposed to finding a snare set and watching it most of the night on September 2, and again the next motning, on Mr. Coward's farm, at the back of Vista Lane. In the morning the defendant took up the snare and witness who knew him well, called out to him and chased him.—Corroborative evidence was given by a little boy named Johnson, and Mr. Coward.—Defendant admitted picking up the snare, but denied having set it. —Fined ss. and costs, and advocate's fee. VETERAN TRADE UNIONIST'S DEATH.

The funeral took place at Billinge on Saturday of the late Mr. T. Berry, of 220, Crow-lane West, Earlestown. The deceased had been ill for about six months, and died last week. A native of Billinge, the late Mr. Berry was an engine-winder, for many years in the employ of Messrs. R. Evans and Co. at Qu€ in and Wood pits, Haydock. He was a member of the Winders' Trade Union and Foresters up till delth. Returning to Earlestown in 1880 the deceased was from that time employed in various capacities at the Waggon Works of the L. & N.-W. Railway. Mrs. Berry pre-deceased her husband in October, 1905. Mr. Berry leaves two daughters, the youngest of whom is unmarried and resided with her father. Much sympathy is felt and expressed for her in the hour of sorrow and bereave- ment, Miss Berry being well-known in the district as teacher at the Wesleyan Schools. Mr. Berry was a member of the Strict Baptist Church, Haydock. BURTONWOOD VICAR ON HARVEST FESTIVALS.

' The Rev. A. M. Mitchell, vicar of Bartonwood, writing in the Parish Magazine, describes the harvest festival as a service of lies. The Church would have done well if she had omitted this undesirable novelty from her services altogether. Much harm is done by services which help to bolster up unreality, falsehood and hypocrisy. The festival never fails to attract a motley crowd of the vuglar, irreligious Godless, and profane, who make this, their service of lies, do duty for a year's attendance. It is an annual agricultural show with parsonio and wardenic eyes (-Ist upon possible underlying dollars. Great cauliflower, monster cabbage, champion potato, huge carrot, swollen turnip are all on view fishing for compliment from Ton-, Dick, and Harry. Withdraw the show and an eager mob of the riff-raff of the Church will vanish. Flesh-eaters make the festival a service of lies ; fruitarians or food reformers are the only consistent worshippers. If we insisted on meats as necessary and God-given, why not adorn the churches with the trophies of butchers, the art triumphs of poulterers, and the fishmongers' marvellous skill?

ST. HELENS.

LODGING HOUSE THEFT.

Joseph Costello was sent to prison at St. Helens, on Saturday, for four days' hard labour on a charge of stealing from a lodging house, at Sandfield-crescent, on Tuesday, August 27. POACHING NEAR ST. HELENS.

William Wainwright, of Thatto Heath, pleaded guilty at St. Helens cn Saturday to night poaching. Mr. T. Garner said that the man was caught by Thomas Rimmer, a gamekeeper for the Earl of Derby, on land in the occupation of Mr. Valentine, at Eccleston, at 230 on Saturday morning. The keeper, who was accompanied by two policemen, caught Wainwright with a newly-killed hare in his possession, as well as a net and other implements. He was fined 40s. and costs.

TRAINING CHILD STEEPLEJACKS.

On Monday, at St. Helens, John Thomao Akiens, professional steeplejack, was charged with causing his three children, aged five, seven, and fifteen respectively to act as steeplejacks, contrary to the Dangerous Per- formances of Childrens Act. The chimney which they had ascended was a hundred and ten feet high, and the manager of the Roughdales Brickworks considered the performance dangerous. Akiens said it had been the custom with his family to train the children from the moment they could walk. As the Bench considered that Akiens had acted under a misapprehension they ordered him to pay costs and bound him over not to permit his children to go up again till they were of legal age. POLICE AND GANIB.ERS.

At St. Helens on Monday a young man named William Bamber, of Avenue C6ttage, Peasley Cross- lane, was charged with street betting, and James Stringfellow was charged with aiding and abetting. Sergeant Acker said that early on Tuesday, the 3rd inst., he secreted himself along with Police-Constable Smith in position to watch the road opposite the Hawk and Back public-hous.), Peasley Cross-lane. Bamber came on the scene at 1'2.15, and until 130 he kept moving backwards and forwards from the roadway to waste land off Peasley Cross-lane. Stringfellow and others acted as scouts, and kept Bam'oer fully inform(' of the movements of the police on street duty in neighbourhood.-13amber was fined 40s. and 19 costs or a month, and Stringfellow 10s. 6d. and costs or fourteen days.

On the arrival on Friday at the Liverpool Landing stage of the steamer Spaniel from Glasgow a man. named William Henry Innes, of 21, Liverpool-road, St. Helens, was handed over to the police on charges of robbing passengers on board the vessel.—lnnes was brought before Mr. Stewart at the Dale-street Police Court on Saturday when the following charges were Ipreferred against him :—(1) Of having stolen 21s. from the person of William Hill ; (2) of haling stolen from the person of Albert Anderson a gold ring and pipe; (3) of hiving been in unlawful possession of an over- coat. According to the stltement of Chief Inspector Holbrook, it appeared that prisoner and prosecutors ''ere steerage passengers on board the Sdaniel, which left Glasgow for Liverpool on Thursday. At about ten o'clock at night Hill fell asleep, and a passenger named Roberts saw the prisoner behaving suspiciously about him. When Hill awoke he went to the refreshment bar and then he discovered that his pockets were empty. Thereupon Roberts told him what he had witnessed. In the second case Anderson also went to sleep, and when he awoke he discovered that a gold ring had been removed from his finger, and that a pipe had be3n taken out of his pocket. The prisoner was arrested by Police.. constable Lawson, and on being searched at the bride- well there were found in his possession the ring and pipe, in addition to 14s. 9id. in money. At this time he had possession of two overcoats, but it was shown that when he boarded the steamer at Glasgow he was wearing only one overcoat. When charged he denied having stolen the 21s. said he had picked up the ring from the deck of the steamer,and that he had bought the overcoat from a married sister in Glasgow. The prisoner was remand( 1 ifor seven days.

PENKETH.

BIBLE CLAS3.—The Penketh Wesleyan Men's Bible Class held their open service on Sunday in the chapel, the chairman being Mr E.C. Parker,who is president of the class. Mr. Davey Smith gave a most edifying address on the life of D. L. Moody. Miss Houghton WAS an ideal soloist. Mr. G. Weaver officiated at the organ. _

IWESLEYAN CHAPEL.—On Sunday special sermona were preached in the chapel, the Rev. A. Cecil Parker preaching at both services. The choir rendered special music, the anthems being, morning "0 taste and see," Miss C. Atherton singing the solo; evening, "Nearer my God to Thee," soloists, Misses A. Bridge and J. Johnson. The collections at both services were in aid of the Guild funds.

GUILD FESTrvAL.—In connection with the Wesley, Guild, a public tea was held in the Wesleyan school— room on Saturday afternoon at five o'clock when 110 sat dl/4.wn to an excellent repast. The tables were beautifully decorated by Mrs. J. Dodds, of Penketh House, and ladies of the various committees of the Guild. After tea the members and friends made their way to the Chapel when at seven o'clock a public meeting took place. The Rev. John Gunnell took the chair and in an inspiring address said what good work the Guild had accomplished since the Rev. A. Cecil Parker, who was present, had inaugurated it and hoped it would in time turn out some useful local preachers. The Caairman then introduced the Rev. Thomas Miller, the new minister for Bold-street, Warrington, who addressed the meeting. Rev. A. Cecil Parker also gave an address. The Chapel choir was in attendance and Miss Carrie Hastings sang "The children's home." The Rev. E. J. Gawne proposed a vote of thanks to the Chairman and speakers, whioti brought the meeting to a close.

WARRINGTON RAGGED SCHOOL The Proposed Labour Home,

The Warrington nagged School and Mission has, under the guidance of its founder, Councillor James Skelland, been doing a noble work amongst the poor during the past twenty years. Another important new departure is now to be undertaken, and in an appeal which is being issued to the public of Warring- ton, the Committee state that they have long felt the necessity that existed for the opening of Institute Homes similar to those which, owing to the generous liberality of the friends of the unfortunate and desti- tute, have been establkhed in London, Liverpool, Man chester, and other centres of population. The ' Committee have secured suitable premises in Bank- street (exactly opposite the Mission Schools) which they purpose opening as a temporary home and workshop for poor -destitute- childrej Jiff& home will be known as the Warrington Ragged. School Labour Home. The employment will at first, ' consist, of wood-chopping, but as time goes on other branches of useful work will be introduced. Upwards of £l,OOO is needed for the purchase of the Labour Home, for its furnishing, and for the satisfactory maintenance of the work to be carried on in con- nection with it; the committee are very sanguine, ia view of the objects, that the necessary moneys will be 'speedily forthcoming. The welfare and the future of- those unfortunate and destitute ones who oft, n through no fault of their own, a unable to find r and are driven in some cases to despair and sorr- into crime, should appeal to all. The subscription list has been open(' donations of £5O each from Mr. Jam'- new president) and Mr. C. E. Parker.

Ak on a Blind Woman

Police Court, yesterday, Annie .-street, Warrington, was charied An aggravated assault on an old blind largaret O'Neil on Thursday afternoon. that the complainant, who is 60 years of J Warrington from Runcorn on Wednesday. jay she was seen by the prisoner, who asked is wanted lodgings. Prosecutris said she was ory comfortable where she was staying, and the ,oner told her that she could use her sofa the house. Eventually prosecutrix went to the prisoner's house, and whilst the old woman was having tea something was thrown in her cup. There were only the prisoner and the prosecutrix in the house at the time, and the latter said," That is not a very kind thing to do." Prisoner then knocked the old woman down, kicked her in the stomach,and also bit her hand. Prisoner also put her hand on the woman's mouth to prevent her shouting. The old woman managed with assistance to get away, and Police-Constable Woolacott arrested the prisoner. Prosecntrix alleged that the prisoner stole a purse which contained ss. 6d. from her, and the raoney was found in her possession. Prisoner denied the assault, and said it was her step- sister that struck the old woman. She said the prose- cutrix gave her ths money to take care of. The Chief Constable said the prosecutrix recognised the prisoner immediately by her voice. It was a most cowardly asaiult, and if the prisoner was sent for trial, she would, in all probability, be sent te penal servitude. Prisoner was sent to gaol for four months with hard labour.

How Mrs. Alleyne was cured

10, St. James-road, Tunbridge Wells. For about six years I have suffered from indigestion, a nd, in spite of doctors' advice and of various remedies which I took, I continued suffering pain, shortness of breath, nausea and headache. T felt run down, I tired easily, and was unable to take much

exercise. Having heard of Iron-Ox Tablets I decided to give them a trial. They have done me an immense amount of good. They have cured the indigestion, have removed all pain and nausea and have made me much stronger. lam able to take long walks now without tiring myself. My general health is much improved, and I feel that I can recommend Iron-Ox Tablets, not only as a real cure for indigestion, but as a splendid tonic also.

(Signed) E. M. ALLEYNE.

Think over this letter, and what it means to you. No doubt Mrs. Alleyne almost despaired of ever being her- self again. Can you imagine anything more dishearten- ing than to try one thing after another to cure the distressing ailments which result from impaired digestion, and to find that relief is as far away as ever? But when Mrs. Alleyne gave Iron-Ox Tablets a trial she soon found an improvement in her health; the pain, shortness of breath, and headaches from which she suffered so much, quickly disappeared, and soon her indigestion was quite cured. If you know the agonies of indigestion, you can readily understand how happy and grateful Mrs. Alleyne was as soon as she discovered the wonderful curative Snd tonic virtues of Iron-Ox Tablets. And her experi- ence points a way to you in which you can restore

health and strength to your digestive organs. Send to your chemist to-day for Iron-Ox Tablets.

PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

To make potatoes white when cooked let them lie (pared) in cold water for two or three hours.

Copper utensils can be kept brilliant- by be- ing polished with turpentine and finely pow- dered bathbrick.

When making a pudding do not forget to put a pleat in the cloth at the top, so as to allow the pudding to swell.

A bowl of quicklime placed in a damp cup- board will tend to dry it. The lime should be renewed every day or two, as it loses its power.

When a pie-dish or anything that is used in the oven gets discoloured or burnt, a piece of emery paper,. bath brick, or even a cinder, will clean it.

When boiling beef add a little vinegar to the water if you wish it to be-tender. To improve the flavour have a bayleaf and some sweet herbs.

Silver if left lying near guttapercha, gets tarnished very quickly. If kept in a pantry where gas is used, it should always be kept well wrapped up in chamois leather.

Henry Watterson says that the oddest and most humourous transposition of the types that ever came within his observation was in a New York paper which used to print its shipping news on the same page with the .obituaries. One morning a long list of re- spectable names in the death announcements were set forth under the marine head, "Passed Through Hell Gate Yesterday."

An editor of a Western exchange recently began worrying about how he would get his shirt on over his wings, after. reaching para- dise. An envious contemporary sarcastically observed that his difficulty would likely be in finding out how he could get his hat on over his horns.

IMPERIAL WORLD'S LEAGUE AGAINST SOCIALISM.

From the " Times" : QOCIALISM and SPORT.—Rich men are away on their moors, yachts, &c., indulging in those expensive forms of sport which are a luxury enjoy- able only by the wealthy, and which would be swept away, with their property, if Socialism pre- vailed. Yet,vvhile they spend scores and hundreds of thousands of pounds on these sports, they are apt to forget those who are carrying on a work against Socialism which will have the effect of pre- serving their property and perpetuating their pleasures and luxuries. A tithe of all that they spend on pure luxury would provide s good income for our work. IMPERIAL WORLD'S LEAGUE AGAINST SOCIALISM, Founder and Director, Rev. George Brooks, Pioneer and Crusader against Socialism for 15 years, 14, The Terrace,- Greenhithe, London. Funds urgently needed. A manufacturer has promised ,E5O if nine others will do likewise. The "Daily News "commenting on the above said: "We must thank the Rev. George Brooks for baying publicly supplied Socialism with one of its best justifications. If Socialism curbs the squandering of large sums of money on unproductive luxuries it is certainly making for the amelioration of our social life. We hope other crusaders will supply as excel- lent arguments for the cause they seek to oppose." Further comment is unnecessary. DRUNKENNESS IN WARRINGTON.

Sir,—Your correspondent writing under the non- de-plume of " Demos " takes me to task for my "teetotal fanaticism " in referring a week before to the drunkenness prevalent in Warrington in what he terms a "simple, foolish and 'amusing" manner. There is every need for a " simple " message in this drink-ridden locality.' But surely the man who is " amused " at 150 drunken people in our streets while filth and squalor reign supreme would be more fitly designated to fill a place with the long-eared hairy tribe. My critic has perhaps (?) abundant intelligence so we may, therefore, allow a little for his absurd outburst. Rowntree and Sherwell, are, I grant, pro- fessed" teachers of men " but aie, unfortunately, like many of the doctors " better at diagnosing the disease than prescribing the Cure." When " Demos " has I digested a few more of these " standard works" let • him adopt logical, sound, and if needs be, " simple " argument. The public will then, perhaps, read his diatribe. In his present pos;tion they are inclined to ignore it. This I should have done, had it not not been for the " helligant ignorance "displayed therein Does he consider he has weakened my case? If so I wish to re-assert my previous statements and decline to Minimise or reduce any particle thereof. He asks me to mention the particular "industrial channels." Does he really think I am going to take up much of your space in quotations and statistics in order to prove my former statements? This I will do when he is man enough to come out of his shell. For the present let him study; systematically the complex social organism from an economic stand- point" We, as "Temperance fanatics" are quite accus- tomed to this kind of abuse. Knowing the source from which it springs we ignore it, and exhibit that spirit of common sense which, in our opponents is sadly lacking. In expressing his "candid opinion" I wonder, and the public wonder, why "Demos" is not "candid" enough to reveal his true identity? Does he pose as a "probable starter" at the next Municipal contest or does he fear that his literary merits would call forth a shower of congratulations and possibly the Freedom of the Borough? I am quite content to " amuse " the public by drawing from the" stock-in- trade" arguments and the "Chestnuts" which are still applicable to the present-day conditions. lam "my brother's keeper "and on his behalf I am prepared to plead despite the efforts of " Demos" to place a stumblint block in his path.---3 am yours faithfully, J. R. GRIFFIT HS, Moore, 18th September 1907.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to'social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. ' M.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

CHRISTIAN SOCIALISM. "Teach me," I said," for I am ignorant; Lead me, for lam blind. Explain the past, With all its errors. Why am Iso low, And you so high ?"

I do not know whether Mr. Charles E. Parker imagines that he possesses all the virtues—he certainly gives me credit for none. The mantle of the sophist has fallen from his shoulders and from the heights of his affected superiority, he descends to "plain speaking." "Plain speaking" is a dangerous and somewhat degenerate habit. It is often an apology for rudeness. Rudeness sometimes leads to retaliation, and retalia- tion would not be becoming for "one who takes upon himself the editorship of a Socialistic column in a Liberal paper." I would remind Mr. Parker of a motto which used to be engraven on the bottom of porter-pots, punch- bowls and drinking-glasses. It ran thus : Two things a man should never be angry at: what he can help, and what he cannot." Mr. Parker may not be conversant with sucl degrading utensils. I can assure him, however, that such is a fact. It was not "detached in some Socialist literature." _ _

Now to proceed with the argument. Lest I forget, I would like to say here that the. use of such a sarcastic sentence as "if be does not he ought to ", is hardly an aid to argument. It serves no good purpose, bat rather sets a bad example. In my last letter, I quoted some words of Frederick Denison Maurice. The words are these: "I seriously believe that Christianity is the only founda- tion of Socialism and that a true Socialism is the necessary result of a sound Christianity." Mr. Parker. says: John Workman knows very well (if he does not he ought to) that the Socialism of the noble band of men who were known as Christian Socialists, Maurice, Kingsley, Ludlow, Neale and Hughes, was something entirely, different from the Socialism he advocates." Again : "Maurice and Kingsley did not teach a single principle peculiar to modern Socialism, but opposed it with a virile force characteristic of the men." And again: "I am inclined to think that John Workman has found this quotation detached in some Socialist literature, and has never realised the import of the term true Socialism 'as used by Maurice. If this be so he has some excuse, but it indicates a carelessness that is scarcely pardonable in one who takes upon himself the editorship of a Socialistic column in a Liberal paper." And yet again: "Kingsley and Maurice are amongst my most valued library friends. Towards the former, I have always felt a sort of ,hero worship. To have the opinion of such friends used to support theories which I know they strongly opposed is not pleasant, and in loyalty to them I wish to clear their name from such association. I have too much respect for the noble band of Christian Socialists to allow their names to be used in support of a system fraught with grave danger to the commonweal." • In this letter,l am going to refute those statements ; at least, I am going to try. Some years ago, Kingsley preached a sermon on the "Message of the Church to the Labouring Man," taking as his text: "The spirit of the Lord, is upon me to preach the Gospel to the poor &c." In it, there are these words " All systems of society which favour the accumulation of capital in a few hands, which oust the masses from the soil which their fore- fathers possessed of old, which reduce them to the level of serfs and day-labourers, living on wages and on alms, which crush them down with debt, or in any wise degrade and enslave them, or deny them a per- manent stake in the Commonwealth, are contrary to the Kingdom of God which Jesus proclaimed." Writing some years afterwards he said: "Associa- tion will be the next form of industrial development, I doubt not, for production ; but it will require two generations of previous training, both in morality and in drill, to make the men capable of it." And again : "As for any schemes of Maurice's or mine, it is a slight matter whether they have failed or not. The failure of a hundred schemes would not

alter my conviction. Font is my belief that not self- interest, but self-sacrific, is the only law upon which human society cern be grounded with any hope of pros- perity or permanence." Now, what I am concerned to know is, how Mr. Parker's plan of " every man getting the fruits of his labour, the whole fruit, and nothing but the fruit " coincides with those opinions, particularly with the last sentence which I have italicised. Mr. Parker says that he " humbly stands on the , same platform with these good men." (the Christian Socialists.) If they were Socialists, he too, is a Socialist. Let us see what their Socialism

meant. It is a significant fact that the quotation from Frederick Denison Maurice: "I seriously believe that Christianity is the only foundation of Socialism, i and that a true Socialism s the necessary result of a sound Christianity" is quite correct. I have before me as I write, a letter which I have taken the piecaution to get , from the Secretary of the London Branch of the Christian Social Union, wherein he states : "It occurs in the second paragraph of a pamphlet called' Christian Socialism which was the, first of the Tracts on Christian Socialism' published in the years 1849-51. This tract was reprinted by the Oxford Branch of the Christian Social Union about 10 years ago, with a Preface by his Honour Judge Hughes, or in other words Tom Hughes . . . . By the way, Maurice anticipates the objection raised by your opponent, and in the next paragraph he says: "The Socialism I speak of is that of Owen, Fourier, Louis Blanc, and of the Englishmen. Frenchmen, Germans, who have fraternised

with them or produced systems of their own." The social systems of these men, which belong tol the first decades of the 19th century, do not recognisel the right to the whole produce of labcur. It may be interesting to know, incidentally, that Robert ON en, the founder of English Socialism, projected a garden city scheme and drew plans for it so long ago as 1817. Now what did these men believe? Dr. Anton Menger, professor of Jurisprudence in the University of Vienna, states: "The Gotha pro- gramme alone, which was adopted by the Congress held from the 23rd to the 26th of May, 1875, and which still forms the basis of the Socialist Movement in Germany, is drawn up with an approach to scientific precision. The essential points of this programme justify us in concluding that the German socialist party regards the right to subsistence, distri- bution according to wants, as the basis of the future social order." - The Gotha programme lays down as a fundamental principle that labour is the source of all wealth and all civilisation, and that generally beneficial labour being only possible through society, the whole produce of labour belongs to society: that is to say, that, work being universally compulsory, every mem- ber of society has an equal right to a share of the product according to his reasonable requirements. The product of labour, then, belongs not to the labourer, follows necessarily from the right to the ince of labour, but to society, and is by society -Ito every individual according to the measure lsonable wants. '-er believes according to his statement, that educe of labour belongs to the individual 'ety. lent that the two versions cannot be -.mins for us an honest endeavour to

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Saved from the Sea."

)With the Devpnshire coast as its picturesque 1 setting and the fortunes of a hardy fisherman and his wife as the central theme, this drama is highly interesting, although on somewhat orthodox lines. The first act is the most powerful, for the hero, Dan Ellington, finds himself on board ship at the mercy of a hitherto unsuspected enemy who, maddened° by jealousy and drink, determines to kill him. He lashes Dan to the mast and imparts the cheerful intelligence that the vessel is stored with explosives which will do their deadly work when he, the enemy, has, got safely away, However, the hero's brave wife turns up at the critical moment and rescues •him from his awful position. A few seconds later a tremendous report is beard, and the good ship " Ocean Waif " is shattered. This scene is very realistically presented, and creates a great impres- sion. It is, however, only one of many sensational incidents, for Dan's wife °happens to inherit a vast fortune, and an adventurer relative of hers schemes to have her put out of the way in order that he may acquire the wealth. The fisherman and his wife pass through trying ordeals, but there is the traditional happy ending. The major share of the acting falls upon Mr. David G. Noble, who takes the role of Dan Ellington with fine dramatic force. Miss Marie Aukland does well as Nancy Ellington, and Mr. George Wallace gives an exceptionally clever study of Jim Weaver.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Mr. and Mrs. William Maclaren's No. 1. Company will next week play Sir Francis Burnand's great Adelphi Drama "Proof," which is known to most local playgoers, and is sure of a warm welcome in Warrington. Mr. Penn ilton, Bernard Elliott, Carl Beechey,Miss Raleigh, ' lma Kurtz, and Miss Beatrice are included in of a strong company. Full houses should ,r all next week at the Royal Court.

0000 DIGESTM

ENSURES

0009 HEALTH.

When your digestion is in sound working order it makes the food you eat ready for assimilation, and all the nutriment it contains goes into your system to become rich, energising blood; each organ, nerve, and muscle is properly nourished, and you cannot well be otherwise than healthy. You feel strong and cheerful ; your brain is active, your nerves steady. - -

But when your digestion is imperfect your food lags in your stomach and begins to ferment and decompose, instead of being digested. The* fermentation creates poisonous acids and wind or gas, which press on the heart and lungs,causing palpitation and difficult breath- ing. The harmful products of decomposition are drawn into your blood, your system is weakened and the way prepared for painful, perhaps deadly, diseases. I Mother Seigel's Syrup is a purely vegetable medicine. I It acts as a digestive tonic, and assists Nature's own processes in digestion and assimilation. Use it on the first signs of stomach disorder and it will quickly put 1 you right and keep you right.

Mrs. Nellie Nicholls, 2, Whitehall Mansions, Archway_ road, Holloway, London, N., writing on April 15th, 11907, says : ; " About a year ago I seemed to get all out of sorts. I My appetite forsook me, and what I ate went against Imy inclination. Then pains in my stomach followed and wind in volumes. Sometimes I had pains in my back too and headaches were always nagging at me. I had no heart for work or anything else. "Well, I had been going on like this for a long time, when friends urged me to try Mother Seigel's Syrup. I did, and soon the pains all ceased, my appetite came back, and I regained my health and strength."

I Mother Seigel' Syrup is a sure and speedy cure for indigestion, biliousness, constipation, wind, headache.

FORTUNATE LIBERAL CLUB.

Few Liberal clubs occupy a more favoured position than that at High Wycombe,of which Earl Carrington is not only the landlord,but the president. His lordship has for many years charged a rental of only ss. per annum , for the premises, which are now being considerably enlarged, chiefly through his munificence.

MY DEAR CHILDREN, I have had a most difficult task this week in deciding the winners in our Holiday Post- cardeCompetition. Such a lot of beautiful cards have been sent that my members must not feel hurt if they have not won a prize. The cards selected are views from Bideford, Stour- port, Scarborough, Chatsworth, Brighton, etc. Boxes of Fry's Chocolate have been sent to the prize winners, viz., Gladys Knight r Willie Rushby, Lily Lunn, J. A. Knight, G. P. Graves, and Ida Bon. I hope each of the winners will send me a letter when they receive their prize. Now, S want to talk to you about our next Competition, which has just started. I can see from the Picture Postcards received in the last Competition that many of you have had a very nice holiday. I want you to tell me a little about these holidays.; just a few lines on a postcard, or in a letter, will do. I shall give some nice prizes to the best three received. I hope to have a good many to pub- lish in these columns. All attempts must be sent during the present month. More short stories are wanted for the Corner. I shall give dainty, little pocket mirrors for the best ones sent me each week. Now, good-bye for another week. Don't forget the "Account of Your Holiday" Essays. Yours affectionately,

MADAME TUSSAUD. The career of the wonderful old lady who used nightly herself to receive the money she made by her famous wax-w_ork exhibition in Baker-street, was one of the most singular. It is said that the late Duke of Wellington fre- quently enjoyed a gossip wilt). Madame Tus- Baud ; and it is not to be wondered at, for she was one of the few people who lived late into the first half of last century who could dis- tinctly remember the great Revolution of "'93," and who knew intimately almost &very per- sonage who figured in it, from the king and Queen clown to Robespierre and the infamous Marat. By birth a member of one of the most illustrious houses of Germany, and the grand- daughter of the famous Field-Marshal von Gresholtz, Madame Tussaud had exceptional opportunities for studying the various char- acters of her time. She was born at Berne, but, losing her parents when quite a child, was taken by an uncle to Paris, and placed under the care of M. Curtius, her mother's brother, one of the leading spirits of the day. He was originally a medical man, but his fame rested chiefly upon his merits as a modeller in wax. In those days it was not permitted for students in the hospitals to mutilate the dead for ana- tomical purposes, and waxen images of the various parts of the human body were fabri- cated in order to facilitate their studies. These Curtius made to perfection; and later an he opened a museum in the Rue St. Honore. Cur- tius was a brilliant conversationalist, and many writers on the French Revolution are inclined 1 to believe that at his hospitable table, at which Voltaire, Rousseau, Franklin, and Lafayette were wont to assemble, the great scheme of the mightiest of social revolutions was planned. His house was a kind of political club, and here his celebrated niece formed relationship with all the famous people of her time. She lived until 1851, being over ninety years of age when she died. To the last she was active, prid her memory never failed her. Her character was a very singular one, and she was a brilliant conversationalist. •As an artist in wax-model- ling she was most skilful. But it is not only as a modeller that she deserves fame; she also painted exquisitely in miniature. Her models are superb; and, perhaps, the best portraits if the leading men and women of the Revolution are those blackened wax masks which hang in the Chamber of Horrors, most of which were taken from life by her, and deserve to be care- fully reproduced. The model of Marat in the bath, taken immediately after assassination, is from her hand, as is also the original cast cf Voltaire. - ISothern gave a dinner-party one evening to about a dozen men. One of the guests; whom we will call Thompson, was late. They had lust sat down to their soup, when a loud ring announced the arrival of-the late Mr. Thomp- son. Sothern hastily exclaimed, "Let us all get under the table. Fancy Thompkn's sur- prise when he beholds a long table devoid of guests." Sothern's love of practical joking was well known, so that the company were not as. tonisted at the proposition, and in a couple of . seconds every man was concealed from view beneath the table. Sothern made a half dive. then resumed his place at the head of the table. Thompson entered, stared, - and exclaimed, " Holloa 1 where are all the fellows?" Sothern shook his head in a lugubrious fashion, and, in melancholy tones, replied, "I can't explain it, my dear fellow; but the moment they heard your name, they all got under the table." The expression on the faces of the hoaxed guests as they slowly emerged, one by one, from their concealment, can be better imagined than de- scribed. A couple of young men went out fishing the other day, and, on returning, were going past a farmhouse and felt hungry. They shouted to the farmer's daughters, "Girls, have you any buttermilk?" The reply was gently wafted back ,to their ears, " Yes ;,but we keep it for our own i calves."

FAIRY TALE, FROM THE GERMAN. (Continued from last week.) O. But while she spoke thus, she thought to her- self, "How can a silly frog that sits in the water and croaks be my companion?" As soon as the frog had obtained her promise, he dipped his head under the water, dived, and in a short time rose again with the golden ball in his mouth, and threw it on the grass. The princess, full of joy at the sight of her pretty toy, seized it quickly, and ran away. "Wait, wait 1" cried the poor frog; "take me with thee; I cannot run so fast 1" All his croak- ing and screams availed nothing, however; she would not listen; but hastened home, and left him to go down again into his well. The next day, when the king and his cour- tiers sat at table, eating from their golden plates —splish splash! splish 1 splash! was heard as- cending the marble steps, and presently some- thing knocked at the door of the banqueting room, and crisd, "Oh, king's daughter! youngest and fairest, open to me I" The prin- cess peeped out, and there sat the frog! Pale, and full of fear, she returned to her place at the table. "Why fearest thou, my child?" asked the king, who saw that she trembled, and that her heart was beating fast and hard; "is there a giant come to fetch thee at the door ?" " Ah, n !" replied she, "not a giant, but a dirty frog that brought my ball out of the water for me yesterday, and so I promised that he should be Illy companion; but I never thought that he could• leave-his well; now ..he is out there, and wants to come in to me!" And again the knocking was heard, and a mournful voice, which said: "Oh, king's daughter! youngest, and fairest, and best! Pray open the door to thy promise-led guest Remember that yester morn, when thy ball fell, 'Twas I who restored it thee from the deep well!" _ "Thou hast given a promise, and thou must abide by it!" cried the king, sternly; "open the door." The trembling princess dared not disobey,-and in hopped the frog; he kept close to her feet, and, when she sat down at the table, cried, "Lift me up, and put thy little plate near me. and let us eat together!' The king commanded her to do all he wished; but, while the frog ate heartily and enjoyed himself, every morsel she swallowed nearly choked her. "I have had enough," said he, at last, " and I am weary, so take me to thy little chamber, and lay me on thy silken bed, and I will sleeP awhile 1" The maiden began to weep, for she did not like to touch the cold, wet creature, nor to lay him on her pretty bed. "Despise not one who has assisted thee in thY time of need," said the king, wrathfully; so she seized the poor frog with two fingers, carried, him' to her •chamber, and put him into a dark corner. But he hopped to her again, crying, " am tired; I want to rest; lay me on the bed, or i will tell thy. father!" The princess angrilY snatched him up, and flung him with all her might against the wall. "Rest there, then. dirty frog!" said she, when behold, instead of the reptile, a prince stood before her ! gig bright eyes looked kindly qn her, as he led her by the hand to the old king, and asked her 'for his bride. He then related in what manner a wicked fairy had changed him into a frog, and had granted to none but the princess the power to set him free. When the sun rose the next morning, there appeared before ,the castle gates a handsome chariot, drawn by eight white horses adorned with plumes of waving feathers, and harnesFed with golden chains. Near it stood faithful Henry, the prince's servant, who had felt such bitter sorrow when his lord was enchanted, that he had been obliged to bind his heart with tiirEe iron hoops, to prevent it bursting with gries:. The happy prince entered the chariot with beautiful bride, to return to his country ail' kingdom once more, and faithful Henry toolt: his seat behind them. They had proceeded but a short way on their journey, when the prince heard something crack; he feared it wa s the chariot breaking; but Henry cried, " !it is but one of the bands I placed round my heart, when my lord was wearily imprisoned in the deep, cold well." Again and again the cracking was repeated—. each time the prince thought the chariot wasi broken; but it was indeed only the bursting the iron hoops that encircled the faithful eel' vant's heart, which were useless now that his lord was free and happy.

There is no like that which springs from a blind ael or a pleasant deed; and you will fee, it at night when you rest, at mornins when you rise, and through the day about your business.

OUR UNSEEN SUN.

No one has even seen the sun. This, says a writer in the Pall Mall Magazine, is not an epigramm-atic pleasantry, but the cheer- less scientific truth. A series of concentric shells aenvelopes a nucleus of which we know absolutely nothing, except that it must be al- most infinitely hotter than the fieroest fur- nace, and that it must amount to more than nine-tenths of the total solar mass. That nucleus is the real sun, for ever hidden from uc.

COUNTY EDUCATION COMMITTEE. ,

Training College Scheme For Cheshire.

ILancashire Education Committee on Monday by , resolution protested against the decision to reduce the Treasury grant to the Manchester University from I £12,000 to £lO,OOO per annum, and expressed the hope i that the, Government would reconsider the matter. The I need for additional training college accommodation in the county was urged, and a resolution was passed calling on the Higher Education Sub-Committee to again consider the question. A TRAINING COLLEG E SCHEME. The Cheshire Education Committee on Monday approved the idea of providing a training college for teachers with a hostel. A special Sub-committee , reported that the training of ex-bursars whileemployed as student teachers, would, in the circumstances of a t county area such as Cheshire, be costly, difficult to work, and ineffective, and that the establishment of a I training college would, as compared with any other system, be of both financial and educational advantage. Professor Gonner, in moving the adoption of the recommendations of the Sub-committee, said that at present training colleges were overwhelmed with appli- cations. A site for a college for 80 or 100 students would cost about £3,000, the cost of the building would be about £6O a head, and the cost of a hostel annexed would be about £3,000. or, altogether, an outlay of about £12,000, towards which the Board of Education would co.ntribute 75 per cent. That would leave the county to provide £3,000, which would involve £212 a year in the repayment of principal and interest for ' thirty years. The entrance fees would be about £lO, the Government would make a maintenance grant towards the cost of the students, and the students would have to provide the difference. They would want !80 places for their own students, and might be able to ; take a few from outside. 1 The Motion was carried unanimously.

LIQUID CRYSTALS.

It is now many years since the interesting discovery was made that certain liquid bodies may possess the optical prdperties of crystals. To these the term "liquid 'crystals" has been applied. If the definition of a crystal as a body bounded by plane faces of regular geometrical form be rigidly adhered to, these doubly refractive liquids could not rightly be so called. Since, however, they possess what may be regarded as the more essential properties of- crystals, viz., their optical characters they may appropriately be classed with them. The "Proceedings of the Royal Society" contains an account by Adams and Aschoff of some experiments on liquid crystals carried out by them. Their ob- servations were made on Myelin bodies, and potential fluid crystals. They find that the Myelin bodies often possess the property of double refraction. On account of this and other characters they must be regarded as fluid sphero-crystals. It was also found that a number of substpces of the nature of soaps possess these properties:

===== 0002601_19070921_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
ANTIQUITY OF THE TSETSE FLY.

If Professor Cockerell is right in his iden- tification, that troublesome pest, the tsetse fly, is of considerable antiquity. In the ' Miocene shales of Florissant, Colorado, a fossil fly was found with a very long and strong proboscis, and on examining it Pro- fessor Cockerell concluded that it must be a ' tsetse. A further scrutiny, and a comparison with figures of flies of the genus Glossina, convinced him that if it could not be in- cluded in this group it was at any rate very nearly allied. A slight difference in the vena- tion of the wing was observed which may or may not be thought of sufficient value to justify the placing of this 'fly in a separate genus. However this may be, the fossil is doubtless to be regarded as a tsetse fly of Miocene Age. Professor Cockerell further identifies the fossil with the species of Oligo- cene Age described by Scudder in 1892. This fly was named Palnestrus oligocenus, and re- ferred to the order ZEstride. Combining these two results we may trace back the an- cestry of the tsetse fly to Olircene times. lAt the present day_ the destructive nature of ! the tsettse is well known.

===== 0002601_19070921_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
DEW - PONDS.

In theit book on "Neolithic Dew-ponds and Cattle-ways," by Dr. A. J. Hubbard and Mr. George •Hubbard, F.S.A. (Longmans), the authors state their conviction that the habitability of large desolate tracts of land in this planet depends to-day upon the adoption of some scientific modification of the prehistoric device which alone, made life possible on the hill-tops to our neolithic an- cestors. It is maintained that over 1,000 slieep may be watezed daily at one of these dew-ponds when it is in good working order, and that every morning finds it replenished. Consider, for instance, they say, the case of the three islands known as the Desertas. Their nearest point is about twelve miles from Madeira, and they are close together; the largest measures about 'ten miles by two miles. As their name implies, they are desert islands, on which we believe that 1 RAIN NEVER FALLS.

LANCASHIRE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON " A " V. ROCHDALE HORNETS" A."— Warrington " A" team opened their season in credit- able style on Saturday, Rochdale Hornets being the visitors to Wilderspool. A, round of passing resulted in Landor again scoring at thel corner. Just before the interval the Hornets scored through Kiley. Half-time : Warrington "A," 14 points : Rochdale Hornets, three points. In the second half Warrington had again the better of matters, tries b6ng registered by Kenyon (2) and Jolley. Final: Warrington, 33 points: Rochdale Hornets, six points.

BROKEN LEG CAUSES DEATH.

The coroner for the honour and fee of Halton has been notified of the death at Runcorn Hospital of William Woodcock, aged forty-six, labourer, of Cooper- street. A week ago Woodcock slipped off thekerb of the wotway in Lord-street and fell. His leg was fractured just above the ankle, and he died as a result of the injnry. The inquest was held on Monday after- noon by Mr. Ridgway, the deputy-coroner. Fred Rovrjes, fitter, said he saw the deceased's feet suddenly fly from beneath him as though he had slipped upon something. Deceased had had some drink, but wap not drunk.— Dr. Delaney said the decet.sed had a compound communated fracture of the leg. Blood-poisoning set in, and caused his death.—A verdict according to the medical evidence was returned.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

By " °vast."

One of the tit-bits of the season is reserved for the Wilderspool patrons to-day. The visit of P.uncorn is always interestipg, and generally productive of a good game. The "Linnets" play to win wherever they go and they would as soon win at Warrington as any- -where else. In times past they could rely upon doing cso with some regularity, but in recent seasons they have always lost points on the Wilderspool ground. TI expect they will be on the wrong side to-day, though I do not attach much importance to the fact that Runcorn have already been twice beaten, whereas "Warrington have won their two opening matches. Runcorn have a good side yet, and ought to do far better than of late.

Warrington will have practically the same team as last week,Lees coming in,in place of Jordan,and Belton :substituting Harmer. Neither Jenkins nor Shugars has returned yet, but they are expected every week now. The Warrington side will therefore be : Tilley, ; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, and Dickenson, threequarters ; Lees and Brooks,half-backs ; Preston, Thomas, Boardman, Heath, Belton and Naylor, forwards.

It was a good performance by Warrington to win at Rochdale on Saturday, for the Hornets are an improved aide and always make a great effort against the Wire- pullers. Warrington's superior class told, however, in the long run and they returned winners by 10 points to 5. The game was a decidedly fast one, and very keenly contested, especially by the forwards. In fact an attempt to secure possession by Proctor of the Hornets resulted in that player sustaining a broken leg in a collision with Thomas. The War- rington forwards were always a bit too powerful for the home lot and generally got possession. A =eh bigger score would have better represented the play, for the Warrington passing twice took the ball to the line where Upton, the full-back, intercepted in remarkable style. He was about the best man on the Hornets' side, though Baxter played a sound game.

The scoring was opened by the homesters, Schofield bluffing the Warrington defence by a sham pass in a fashion which delighted the 6,000 people who patronised the Hornets opening match. Warrington had the wind against them, but smart work behind resulted in Fish getting away and feeding Harmer who crossed near the goal, Fish completing the effort with a neat place kick. A. penalty goal by Lipton brought the scores level at the interval. On resuming, Warrington soon began to look a winning side, the backs playing well though closely watched by the home defenders. Brooks cross-kicked cleverly, and Taylor racing up beat the fall back and gav9 Dickinson a walk in, Fish again goaling. The scdre ought to have been augmented by the winners, but several chances were missed.

On the Warrington side the forwards and halves won the game. Both worked hard, and Jordan and Brooks came in for some hard knocks. Brooks played with cleverness and dash, and frequently got through The defence. Taylor, Fish, and Tilley all did well again, while little fault could be found with Dickinson and Isherwood.

F. G. Bate was the most successful batsman in Mr. Podniore's eleven. He played Frank Harry's bowling with confidence and-carried out his bat after compiling 27. B. Lowe was also in good form and made 20. The same player shone in the bowling department, capturing seven wickets. Sergeant Milward (14) was the only other player to reach double figures. W. H. Podmore, the captain,l had decidedly hard luck, being caught off Harry's first ball by Jones. The match was a pleasant and interesting one and the Guardians are to be con- gratulated upon their victory.

The championship of the Warrington and District League has been won by St. James', who trounced Cheshire Lines in no half-hearted style. Batting first the Engineers made a feeble stand against the deliveries of Holt, the side being dismissed for the small score of 28. St. James' hit off the runs for the loss of one wicket and their innings realized 91. Foster and Holt batted well for 23 and 13 respectively. C. Holt has secured the prize for the best batting average with 294 runs for 13 completed innings.

Cricket.

The summer sport has now made its exit and on the whole Warrington have reason to congratulate them- eelves on the doings of the season which closed with a brilliant victory over Ashton-under-Lyne. It was, of ourse,a great disappointment not to gain the champion- ship of the West Lancashire League. but individual members of the eleven have done splendidly in the competition matches, J. W. Bibby securing the gold medal for the best batting average and Cresswell, the professional, being the winner of a similar medal for the best bowling analysis. The long spell of bad weather interfered a great deal with the sport, and only 16 matches were played, of which eleven were won , -four lost, and one drawn. The games lost in the League ' were the two with St. Helens, whilst in the ordinary contests Wigan got the better of our local eleven after ' most exciting finishes. The wet season has been res- ponsible for low scoring. Warrington have compiled 1.756 for the loss of 138 wickets, which works out at an average of 12.44 per wicket. Against this Warrington have dismissed 158 opponents at a cost of 1,060 runs. Thus showing an average of 6.71 runs par wicket. The bulk of the bowling has been done by Cresswell and H. Callon, and both, as will be seen from the appended figures, have exceedingly creditable analyses.

Bowling.

Local trundlers were greatly interested in the final for the Warrington Works Competition which was decided on Saturday at the Bowling Green, Bank Quay. The contestants were Whitecross No. 1 and Sankey Wire Mills and the first named were favourites as they defeated Pearson and Knowles' team, last year's champions, in the fourth round. Anticipations were realised, Whitecross winning eight individual games and finishing with a majority of twenty-five points.

To deal with individual games, W. and J. Gleave were the first to appear on the green. W. Gleave had a run of successes to the fourth end when J. Gleave got in a couple of points. After this the games were pretty evenly contested until the eighth end when W. Gleave got ahead and maintained his position to the close, winning by 15-11.

Ted Gleave easily defeated T. Forster. At the seventh end Forster was leading by 5-4 but at the next attempt Gleave drew level and then had matters all his own way, winning by 15-5.

There was a close contest between 3 ohn Gleave and Rutter. The first named had hard lines in being defeated, for at the twelfth end he was leading 10-6. Gleave was a doughty opponent and drew level at 12-12 and ultimately triumphed by 15-13.

There was an interesting contest between Jones and Harrison—the first-named leading throughout and winning by 15-1 L E. Gleave gained a hollow victory at the expense of Jennings. He was always leading, and ran up a majority of eleven.

The game between Massey and W. Gleave was pretty close for a time, but at the sixth end Massey asserted himself, and his opponent had not a look in, being defeated by 15-7.

T. Harrison and Horton were well matched. At the ninth end they were level with 6-6. Horton then had a spell of luck, but Harrison was not to be denied, and gradually catching up to his opponent the players were on equal terms with fourteen each. Harrison secured the winning end.

The game between Fletcher and Warburton at one time promised to be very keen, each contestant having placed seven to his credit, but at the seventh end Warburton got is front and held the lead to the close, winning by 15-9.

Wilcox had hard lack in being beaten by Cooper, for he led up to the twelfth end. Cooper then gave a taste of his quality and ranniit winner by 15-10.

The most one-sided game of the match was between Wisedale and A. Gleave, the latter being victorious by 15-3.

Shelmerdine made a good fight against Siddall and led 9-6, but failed to obtain any more points and was beaten by 15-9.

There was a capital game between W. Forster and Ellison, the players repeatedly drawing level. When 10-10 was reached Ellison got the upper hand and was victorious by 15-10.

At the conclusion of the game the cup and medals were presented by a vice-president of the Association, Mr. J. Wiilett, who expressed his regret at there being nobody else to officiate. Their President, Captain Crosfield, was away on his holidays. Their Association had now completed its fifth year. The first winners were L. & N.-W., the second P. & K., the third Rylands Bros., the fourth P. & K., and he was glad to say the fifth Whitecross. He hoped that next year the Wire Mills would try an,' wrest the cup from them.

Mr. E. Scott (secretary) said the Association was perhaps one of the best of its kind in England, when they considered that in a comparatively small town like Warrington, there were 33 teams representing different works in the Association. The subscribers had also been very liberal, and they had been able to buy 53 prizes for the individual competition. (Hear, hear.)

Mr. R. Straw proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. Willett for presiding, and for presenting the medals, and it was carried unanimously.

The winners and runners up afterwards adjourned to the Bowling Green Inn for tea, which was provided by Captain Crosfleld. An excellent repast was served, after which a smoking concert was held.

ON THE SHIP CANAL.

A Visit of Inspection.

The annual in.spection of the Ship Canal on behalf of the Manchester Port Sanitary Authority took place on Saturday. The Port Sanitary Authority came into being when ships from other lands began to visit the youngest of British ports. It is a body charged with the protection of the health of all the crews of the vessels that enter the canal. It sees, also, that the food stuffs which come in so many ships are sound and fit for human use before they are despatched inland. The work of the authority is of necessity carried out by its officers, but once a year its members have the oppor- tunity themselves of seeing the waterway. On Saturday the company was smaller than usual. Mr. Alderman Walton Smith, of the Manchester Cor- poration, who is chairman of the Authority, was absent. Mr. Councillor Huddart, of the Salford Corporation (deputy chairman), was there, and with him were Mr. Alderman Meakin (Salford), Mr. Councillor Marsden (Manchester), Mr. Councillor Evans (Warrington), Dr. Dearden (medical officer), and Mr. Arthur Holmes (clerk). The Mayors of Warrington and Widnes (Mr. Alderman Smethnrst and Mr. Councillor S. Owens) and some other representative of the administrative authorities of the districts through which the canal runs, were amongst the guests. Dr. Sergeant, medical officer of health for the Lancashire County Council,was another guest, and the Ship Canal Company were repre- sented by Mr. F. A. Eyre, secretary, and Mr. Hubert Congreve, assistant engineer. BITTING. AGAINST. Alderman Bolton Rev. Father Wilson Mr. Jolley-3

Councillor NIELD said he would like to know how it was that the eirls did not gain admission to the school. Were they unfortunate in the ballot, did they figure at the bottom of the list, or were their religious views a barrier ?

Councillor WILKINSON said the girls had passed the qualifying examination, and it was not their fault, but the fault of the authorities, owing to the lack of accommodation, that they were not able to enter a college. It was niggardly on the part of the authority to wish to pay the girls £7 less in the third year than they were paid in the second year. The DIRECTOR said it ought to be definitely under- stood that there was no top and no bottom in the pre- liminary certificate examination. They were all marked " pasf4 "and if they reached a certain standard in certain subjects they were marked with distinction; the decision as to the admission to the training colleges rested entirely with the authorities of the colleges. The number of teachers who applied for admission was greater than there Nvas room for, and the governors made their selection on the result of the examination, after a personal interview, and on the reports of the headmasters of the pupil teachers' centres. It was therefore not quite the lottery that it had been repre- sented. _

_ nr. it T. FAIRCLOUGH said that before the Secondary School was established, teachers who did not secure admission to the colleges always received the same salary from the managers. At that time, however, they taught in the schools all the week and the mistresses gave them instruction. He could not understand why the teachers in question should continue attending the Secondary School and only teach half their time. Why conli not they teach full time as under the old system?

The DIRECTOR: They continue attending the classes at the request of the parents, to enable them to stand a better chance of passing the examination. Mr. PAIRCLOUGII : But they have passed. The DIRECTOR: Yes, but they are going to sit again and get some distinctions. Councillor PEMBERTON' said that if these girls chose to go elsewhere they could earn £45 a year as uncertificated teachers. Their parents, however wished them to attend the classes and again sit for examina- tion, and why should the committee pay for that? It ought to be stated hefore the examinations hcw many vacancies were to be filled. If the committee inaugur- ated the system of paying the teachers the full amount, they would be tying their hands with regard to the future.

Wesleyan Sunday Schools. Local Returns.

The thirty-second annual report of the Wesleyan Methodist Sunday School Union, which is about to be issued, states that there are now 7,566 Wesleyan schools, with 133,108 officers and teaehers,and 1,000,819 scholars. Eighty-one new schools have been opened, and sixty- two have been closed daring the year. Four districts —Liverpool, Whitby and Darlington, Third London,and Isle of Nian—report increases of 1,958,whi1e the remain- ing thirty-one districts report decreases of 14,530, making the net decrease 12,572. This is the first year sirwn Mu tu tfle Lutat uwut,t,i or scholars has been reported. These unsatisfactory numerical returns are explained partly by the extra- ordinary growth of the Brotherhood movement, and partly by the fact that a new method of filling up the circuit schedules has been adopted for the first time this year. In the Liverpool district there are now 322 schools with 6,470 officers and teachers and 53,500 scholars, an increase of 1,328 on the year. The average attendance is 39,013, an advance of 965. The annual cost of the schools is £4,626, and they raise £4,757 for home and foreign missions. The following are the latest local returns : Circuit. Schools. Teachers. Scholars. ===== 0002601_19070921_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Methodist Free Church, Buckley.street.

There were large congregations at the harvest thanks- giving services at this Church on Sunday when the preacher was Mr. John Upson. In the evening the church was packed,forms having to be brought from the school and placed in the aisles of the church:En the afternoon a service of song was rendered by the choir entitled The glad harvest ti me," the conductor being Mr. Nathan Cook, and the organist Miss M. Plinston. The singing was admirable, and reflected the greatest credit on the choir. The part reading was supplied by Mrs. Moors, and a great measure of the success of the festival was owing to her efficient help in this direction. The decorations were very tasteful, and all agreed that this harvest far surpassed all previous festivals of a similar kind in connection with this church. The collections amounted to £4 is. 81d., exceeding by 13s. 50. the previous year's amount.—A fruit banquet was held on Monday evening, when over 300 paid for admission, the schoolroom being almost full. A splendid entertain- ment was gone through under the chairmanship of Mr. Ernest A. Sanders, the following artistes contributing to the success of the evening :—The Misses M. A. Bate, A. Whalley, N. Lomax, J. Harrison, and Messrs. A. Palmer, NV. Allen and E. Edwards. The accompanist was Miss M. Plinston, and the humorous element was in the hands of Mr. S.Crampton, his contributions being received with continual laughter and merriment. At the close of the entertainment all received a bag of fruit, and a goodly portion was sent (with a bunch of flowers) to all the sick members of the church. St. Barliabas' Church.

The harvest thanksgiving services in connection with this church commenced on Thursday evening when a large congregation assembled. The church had been very artistically decorated with the usual harvest products, the altar, font and pulpit receiving special attention. The service was of a festal character and included the anthem "Bless the Lord" (Simper), the soloists being Messrs. T. Evans, jun., and J. Green. Mr. T. Evans was at the organ. A powerful sermon was preached by the Rev. J. E. Jelly, vicar of St. Luke's, Lancaster, who took for his text the wards: "For He maketh His sun to rise on the evil and on the good, and sendeth rain on the just and on the unjust." (Matthew v., 45). He said the processes of nature were entirply impartial. There was nothing in them to ditcriminate between the good and the evil amongst men; neither were religious views taken into account. No peculiar blessing rested upon the growth of the Protestant potato or the Roman Catholic corn. All we needed to do was to sow the seed and nature would do the rest without asking what kind of men we were. This might be a familiar fact, but when we thought upon it, did it not puzzle and confound us? Did it not make our views of this world and the life upon it very irreligious and mechanical? Of course, if nature was nothing but a vast, self-acting machine, if there were no Will or Person behind nature, directing the workings of the machine, these familiar facts would be easy to understand. The harvest would he a proof of what had been called the grand indifference of nature. Apart from God, nature cared nothing for our puny dreams of good and evil. She was a system of forces acting according. to fixed laws. But as soon as we could see God, as we only could by faith,behind all, the moment we believed that the marvellous procession of seasons, of causes and effects, was being directed and controlled by a supreme Being who was almighty, all- wise, all-loving, whose will was being manifested in the bursting buds of spring and the ripening fruits of autumn, this indifference became incomprehensible. Yet it could not be that God did not care whether we were wicked or righteous, pure or foul. In this impartial treatment of all, God would teach us His fatherhood in its widest aspect. He would mani- fest that He loved all His children alike and made ample provision for them, whether they deserved it or not. When we could see God providing for all His children with equal hand we ought to be drawn more closely to one another in brotherly love, as being children of the same Father. Our artificial life whichj followed so inevitably in the

MR. H. B. IRVING at the Theatre Royal Manchester

The return visit to Manchester next week of Mr. Irving is of more than ordinary interest, as in addition to appearing in those favourites "The Bells." "Charles the First" snd "The 7,y0n5 Mail," he will also produce for the first time on any stage by himself that famous play "Louis XI" and a new farce in which he will also appear, entitled the "Lion and the Unicorn" by Laurence Irving and Tom. Hesslewood. This is a pro- gramme of exceptional strength. With the story of "The Bells" (Sir Henry Irving's version), most play- goers are familiar, with its pleasant home life, the haunted fear of the burgomaster, and the wonderful working of the mind as brought out in the dream, with its imaginary trial and sentence. In front of "The Bells" will be enacted "The Lion and the Unicorn" in which Mr. H. B. Irving will appear as "Charles the Second," and Mr. Irving is also playing "Charles the First." Manchester playgoers will have the unique chance of seeing him as both "Charles the First" and " Second" in the same week, On the Wednesday The Lyons Mail" will be performed with Mr. Irving in those famous dual roles of Joseph Lesurques (the innocent merchant) and " Dubose " (the debauched criminal and murderer) two characters so widely different as to seem almost impossible of pourtrayal by the same actor. On Thursday and Friday evenings and Saturday matinee Mr. H. B. Irving will appear as" Louis XI " for the first time. This revival is, under the circumstances,one of the most welcome changes in his programme, and is in keeping with a promise he made in Manchester during his visit in May last, when he promised to revive from time to time famous plays from the late Sir Henry Irving's repertoire. This will be mounted in the gorgeous manner of the times and on the same scale as on Sir Henry's last visit to Manchester.

WIFE'S SISTER BILL. This highly contentious measure promises to raise a storm of hurricane character throughout the country. in more denominations than one. One side claims .thet there is an evil to be cured, the other side retorts with a blank negative and says that the parties clearly know, or knew, their precise legal standing. A sluggish or cirrhnus liver, however, admits of no contention : the evil is too pronounced, end it is as well to be provided with a remedy which will stimulate the semi-dormant organ and restore its owner to the enjoyment of life and the capability of profitable work. A good home remedy like the following ought not to be overlooked : —Tincture of Buchn, one ounce; garox Compound,one ounce; Syrup of Lemon, two ounces. This plragraph should be cut out and preserved. Any chemist will supnly the ingredients. and they can be mixed at home if desired. Take a teaspoonful after every meal and at bedtime.

It bits been decided by the Master Bakers' Association whose operations embrace a large portion of Mid- Cheshire to increase the price of bread by one half- penny per four-pound loaf. A goods train on the Midland and Great Northern Railway was derailed at Dodsthorpe Crossing, near Peterborough,on Thursday morning,and fearteen trucks went off the rails and blocked the up and down lines.

A conference was held on Tuesday between the governors of the Gramms,r School a,ud the School Management C )mmittee of the Warrington Education Committee to discuss the new regulations of the Board of Education with regard to the Secondary \ -Education. The effect of those rules is that the higher grant will only be paid if the majority of the governors of the school are nominated by representative bodies and 25 per cent. af free places are provided. We understand that the matter was debated in an informal way but no definite decision was arrived at. The regulations will again be considered at the next Meeting of the School Management Committee. Till Robbery at Warrington. Prosecu- tor had occasion to leave his shop for a few minutes last evening, and on his return he found the prisoner leaning over the counter with his hand in the till. The Only Black-Lister. John Wilfrid Delooze, who has the unenviable distinction of being Warrington's only black-lister, again appeared in the dock at the Borough Police Court, on Monday charged with drunkenness. It was Stated by the Chief Constable that the prisoner's con- victions now totalled forty-three. A few day's ago he was locked up on a similar charge, but on promising to reform he was bound over. It was such men as the prisoner who helped to swell the crime record of the town, thereby giving it a bad name.—Delooze was ordered to find sureties and pay the costs, or undergo six months' imprisonment. A Rough Prisoner. _At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Miles having been labourer, Ellesmere-street, was charged with been drunk and disorderly in Buttermark-et- street on Saturday night, assaulting P.C.'s Sankey and Azrcistrong,damaeing the latter's trousers to the extent of 13s. He pleaded guilty to being drunk and disorderly and tearing the trousers,but said he knew nothing about the assa,ult. —P.C. Sankey said he was on duty at 7.15 On Saturday night in Buttermarket-street, in corn- 15anY with P.C. Armstrong. They saw prisoner cirunk, and heard him use very had language, They cautioned him but as he did not desist they took him into custody. He then became very 'violent. He kicked out right and left like a madman and kicked witness and Armstrong about the body Beveral times, and also tore Armstrong's trousers. They obtained the assistance of Sergeant Barnett, and took prisoner to the Police Station. They were "ruggling with him on the ground for about 201 minutes. P.C. Armstrong corroborated, and said Prisoner struck him in the face and kicked him several times in the stomach. He also got hold of witness's trousers (produced), and tore one of the legs from top to bottom. —Prisoner was fined ss. and ordered to pay the damage to the trousers and the Court costs (V 6d. altogether.)
