Warrington had to , GEORGE EADE, 232, Goswell road Londo.n GEORGE EADE, 232, Gosw-ell road, London. make the journey without S. Lees, who had a bad cold, ' and Jordan filled the vacancy. Play was of a fast "-- #4;ADP7 S GOUT 8c RHEUIVIATIC PILLS• nature throughout, and the only thing which marred a;,.. ,a/ the match was an accident to Proctor, one of the home forwards, who sustained a broken leg in a collision with Thomas. The weather was fine but windy, and War- rington had the elements against them in the first half. Teams : Warrington: Tilley, back ; Fish, Isherwood, Taylor, and Dickenson, three-quarter backs; Jordan and Brooks, half backs; Thomas, Harmer, Heath, Naylor, Preston, and Boardman, forwards. Rochdale Hornets: Upton, back ; Hartley, Williams, Ward, and Richardson, three-quarter backs; Schofield and Baxter, half backs; Taylor, Proctor, Crabtree, W. Richardson, Turner, and Dearden, forwards. Referee, Mr. H. Lowe, Broughton. Touch judges, Messrs. H. Broome, Oldham, and J. H. Cronshaw, Salford. Preston started the game and even play followed at centre. Eventually Warrington set up a strong attack, judicious kicking foiling many attempts of Upton and Richardson to clear. A passing movement between Brooks and Isherwood provided Fish with an easy chance to open the scoring, but the wing men missed the ball unaccountably. Clever work by the Rochdale backs gave them the advantage, and though Fish and Taylor eased the pressure slightly, another return to the attack was attended with success, for Schofield bluffed the opposition, and scored a good try. Hartley missed the goal. Rochdale pursued the aggressive and mulling endangered Warrington's goal until Fish got clear and forced matters beyond the half-way line. The Warringtonian was again prominent with good work, and this time he passed to the forwards. The ball was dribbled over, and Harmer scored near the posts. Fish placed the goal.and thus secured Warrington the lead. Rochdale pressed and from a good position Upton had a shot at goal. The ball struck the upright and careered between the posts,thus placing the sides on level footing. Rochdale,aided by the wind, still pressed but met a defence set up by Tilley, Taylor and Brooks, which baffled all efforts to cross. Next came a fine round of passing by the Warrrington backs, but Upton intercepted, and saved the Rochdale line at a most critical moraent. The home custodian cleared to centre field, only to see Isherwood return to the attack, and provide Warrington with a good opening. The chance was allowed to slide. A penalty against the visitors afforded Rochdale relief, and then a mull by Tilley endangered his line. Fish cleared and Hartley next tackled Taylor when he appeared likely to cross. Half- time : Warrington, one goal one try (five points) ; Rochdale Hornets, one goal one try (five points). The early stage of the second half witnessed the visitors hard pressing, and Fish effected a fine run along touch until checked by Richardson. The visitors maintained the pressure, but the defence proved safe until Upton was beaten by a cross-kick by Brooks, and Taylor securing possession, passed to Dickenson, who raced over behind the posts. Fish placed the goal. The serious accident to Proctor robbed the game of any further interest, and the final result was as follows: —Warrington, two goals two tries (ten points) ; Roch- dale Hornets, one goal one try (five points).

Farm Fire. A serious fire occurred on the farm of Edward Cannon, Lower Whitley, on Monday night. Constable Dutton discovered that a fifty-ton stack of clover was well alight, and gave the alarm. The fire had got a good bold, and the blaze could be seen over four miles , away. The Warrington Fire Brigade was summoned, but could not obtain water, and the stack was completely burnt out. The damage is estimated at £2OO. The stack burnt until seven o'clock next morning. THE EXPERIMENTAL DOG. At Runcorn on Monday several persons were summoned for allowing their dogs at large between sunset and sunrise, and Dr. NeVinstein, of the Crofton Lodge of the Liverpool School of Tropical Medicine, was one of the defendants. He told the bench that the dog was used for experimental work, and not kept for pleasure.—Mr. Littler (chairman) : We shall be obliged to fine you 2s. sd. The public would be rather more afraid of your dogs than anyone else's, although I ! don't think they would do any more harm. (Laughter.)

Sutton, the hon. secretary, had quite ; THE FUTURE OF THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL AND a flattering reception on going to the wicket but he had s FAIRFIELD SCHOOL. the misfortune to be dismissed for a " duck." W. C. I Brooks the captain, was bowled first ball by Lowe. It having been reported that the number of scholars , ,in attendance at the upper girls' department of the Fairfield school was considerably below that for which the department was staffed, the School Management Committee resolved that notice be given to Miss Edith G. Parsonage, the certificated assistant mistress who was appointed to this department before the mid- summer holidays conditionally upon the number of children attending the school after the holidays justifying it, terminating her engagement on the 31st October, 1907. The question of the maintenance or otherwise of two girls' departments at the Fairfield school as separate departments was again considered, and the Director was instructed to invite the managers of the school to furnish the committee with any reasons they may desire to submit in support of the continu- ance of the present arrangement. A list of the scholars to be admitted to the Branch Technical schools during the present session, free of charge, on the recommendation of the head teachers of the public elementary schools, was approved. The Committee also granted free admission to the Secondary School to three scholars. It was noted that a special meeting of the Committee would be held on the afternoon of Tuesday, the 17th September, to meet the Governors of the Grammar School to discuss with them the new Regulations of the Board of Education for Secondary schools. Mr. UPSON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that probably at the next monthly meeting the school Management Committee would have something definite to submit with regard to Fairfield Schools. They were faced with the difficulty of having in the one building two schools, one of which was continuously declining in numbers, and they had to consider whether, educationally and financially, it was a good thing to maintain the two separate departments in the one building. However, they were waiting for a communi- cation from the managers and no doubt a decision would be arrived at after that had been \ considered by the School Management Committee. With regard to the free admissions to the Secondary School and Technical Institute, the Committee were continuing the policy which they had adopted from the first, that every child who had the capacity to benefit by secondary or technical education should have the opportunity of gaining that training. They had always believed that no child should be debarred by the circumstances of his home or by the poverty of his parents from profiting by the educational advantages which the committee could place at his disposal. The only thing they asked was that these children should show their capacity by passing entrance examinations and should show further by their diligence and progress that they were entitled to a continuance of the advantages. Alluding to the conference which had been arranged with the governors of the Grammar School, Mr. Upson said that by reason of the issue of new regulations governing Secondary Schools, the com- mittee were faced with a problem which they must solve in the immediate future. As far as the Grammar School was concerned, the new regulations provided two scales on which grants would be paid and the higher grant would only be paid if the majority of governors of the school were nominated by representa- tive bodies, and on the provision in the Grammar School and all Secondary Schools of 25 per cent. of free places. It had been thought that by a conference with the governors of the School, part, at any rate, of the problem might be solved atid no doubt by the next monthly meeting there would be something definite to place before the committee. Father WILSON seconded and the minutes were approved. THE FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN. MR. JOLLEY AND PARENTAL RESPONSIBILITY. In connection with the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1906, a report was submitted to the School Attendance Committee as to the number of children attending the schools in the Borough who are stated to be insufficiently fed, and, after careful consideration of the fa.cto ouhmitted, it was resolved that application be made to the Board of Education for permission to adopt the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1906. Councillor ELLISON moved the adoption of the minutes. Mr. E. PLINSTON, in seconding, said he would like to strengthen the application for the simple reason that the question was likely to be an important and difficult one, because they knew well that a number of people were quite satisfied to squat about the streets of Warrington, getting all the drink they possibly could and doing nothing for their children. It would be very difficult to differentiate between those and the class of children who, through no fault of their own or of their parents, were suffering and required the meals that were requisite before they could study. Alderman BOLTON moved that the minute be not approved. He had no objection to a child having a penny cake or even a good breakfast or anything of that description, but he had a decided objection to the employment of an extra staff which would make every penny cake cost 30s. for delivery. The minute ought to be sent back until tbe committee had full particulars as to what the adoption of the Act would mean. He did not think every school should be bothered with a particular class of breakfast for one section of the children. It would make the others dissatified and would bring the school more to the class of the pauper and the Workhouse. He had no objection to one school doing it, or to all for that matter, but he had an objection to putting in force an Act that would put a large staff in the schools to provide free breakfasts for the children of lazy people. Mr. JOLLEY seconded and contended that there was nothing in the local circumstances to justify the application. He submitted with all respect that the existing means of relieving real destitution were quite adequate. Whether they considered the Poor Law or the efforts of voluntary charity, in the present con- dition of the town there was no reason for the appli- cation. Further he contended that whether the question was looked at from the standpoint of the scientific sociologist or of the practical Christian worker, it was equally open to condemnation, because ' the net effect directly undermined or tended to undermine parental responsibility, and indirectly it was the means of pay- ing out of the rates a premium on drunkenness, laziness and profligacy. Councillor WILKINSON did not agree with Mr. Jolley that provision was made to the fullest extent for destitute people. When children were ill-fed it was the duty of the authorities to endeavour to build up ' their constitutions so that they might be able to bear the mental strain of education. When they had adopted the Act they could hold meetings to consider the course that they were going to take. They had their remedy always, for if they found that drunken parents who were in work and able to keep their children did not ; provide for them, they could take steps to have the money refunded. I Mr. FL ROBERTS said he could not conceive any reasonable objection fro the adoption of the Act. The time would come when those who were responsible for patting it into force would have to decide whether to put it into force or not. It would be quite time then to discuss the objections. Alderman Bolton, who moved the amendment with a remarkable absence of logic or anything else, made certain objections which were characteristic of his usual style of argument. Beyond all controversy, there were in every populous district a number of children who were sent to school in a condition thoroughly unfit to receive education. Mr. JOLLEY: Through whose fault? Mr. ROBERTS: The question as to whose fault it is does not enter into the consideration of the subject at all. Mr. JOLLEY: It ought to. Mr. ROBERTS : That is just the point where we differ. The child in the first instance has a right to be fed. As to who is responsible for feeding it is another matter. You talk about interference with parental responsibility. You are doing it every day of the week. You interfere with parental responsibility when you require the parent to send his child to school. Why shouldn't he hive as much right to keep the child away from school as to starve it ? If he has a right to starve it physirilly, he has a right to starve it in mental requirements. There is no logic in saying he can send a child to school with an empty belly when he cannot keep it away from school if he wants. Continuing, Mr. Roberts pointed out that the Act was permissive and if they got power to use it, then would come the time for those who pro- posed its adoption to show a concrete case. The Act would loin the authorities to bring home the reap'.ncihitity of parents far more satis- factorily than at the present time. What did they depend on with regard to the punishment of parents who neglected their children? A voluntary association .unnorted by public contributions. It was quite tree that the law helped them in the matter. but who bad to net the law into operation ? The officers of the N.S.P.C.C. That was the institution which this great country of ours depended upon almost absolutely
